Intent Data Is A Gift, With Edible Brands

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Edible Arrangements sells 350 SKUs on its website year-round. But for Valentine’s Day and on Mother’s Day, only around 10% of them are available to buy.

That might sound like a counterintuitive move for a retailer on its busiest days of the year. But Edible shrinks its catalog on purpose, says Erica Randerson, chief digital officer at Edible Brands, which owns Edible Arrangements, on this week’s episode of AdExchanger Talks.

The logic is based on shopper behavior. Most gift shoppers, as it turns out, became terrible procrastinators during the pandemic.

Pre-pandemic, many Edible orders arrived a week or even 10 days ahead of a holiday, Randerson says. Now, most come in during the final 48 hours – and those shoppers know what they want, because they’re in a time crunch.

At peak times, on-site conversion rates can hit between 10% and 15% compared with the 3% or 4% that’s typical for ecommerce, a difference Randerson attributes to the high intent that gift buyers bring.

So Edible gets out of their way. Fewer choices means a faster path to checkout and a more manageable load for the franchisees who make and deliver every order. The stakes are high, though. Valentine’s Day and Mother’s Day together drive roughly 40% of Edible’s annual orders.

But gifting data also has a quirk that most ecommerce data doesn’t. Every order has two customers, the buyer and the recipient – and knowing both is a major advantage, Randerson says.

Edible won’t market to a recipient, however, until it gets explicit permission.

“A customer actually cannot even opt their recipient into communications,” Randerson says. “That mechanism doesn’t exist.”

Recipients can opt in themselves, though. Every Edible gift package comes with a hang tag that has a QR code, and if the recipient scans it, they land on a page offering a free gift in exchange for their email address.

That’s a rather traditional albeit effective approach for gathering first-party data. But there’s also a newer source of intent arriving from a very different place: AI search.

Although chatbots currently send less than 10% of traffic to the Edible Arrangements site, those visitors tend to convert better and spend an average of $10 more per order – which “is not insignificant,” Randerson says.

“It’s clear that these channels are helping convert our customers before they get to us,” she says.

Also in this episode: how Edible Brands decides what tech to build in-house versus what to buy off the shelf, how it’s planning for the chaos of Valentine’s Day – which lands on Super Bowl Sunday this year! – and the origin of Edibles.com, its marketplace for hemp-derived THC products. Plus: Randerson’s former life as a competitive clogger.

Criteo has weathered considerable storms in its day, including a painful moment in March 2020 when its market cap bottomed out below $500 million. Can it pull off yet another turnaround?

Disaffection and outright anger toward US sports institutions run deeper than logo-cluttered fields. Leagues and teams are sacrificing player safety and fan enjoyment for marketing dollars.

People Inc.’s referral traffic from Google search is down 70% over the past two years, and by 26% in Q3 alone. But its digital revenue is still growing, albeit slowly.

On Tuesday, Chord, a six-year-old commerce tech startup, acquired Zenlytic, an AI startup with an anthropomorphized data analyst agent named “Zoë,” for $5 million.

If you ask the executive leadership of Affle and Perion why the two companies are merging, the answer you’ll get is “synergies.”

A combo of Tubi, sports and political ads powered Fox’s growth this last quarter, even as Fox News lost viewers and the Roku deal awaits DOJ clearance.

AdExchanger is where marketers, agencies, publishers and tech companies go for the latest information on the trends that are transforming digital media and marketing, from data, privacy, identity and AI to commerce, CTV, measurement and mobile.

These classic design fails are still making us laugh

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

There are some important lessons to learn from these 15 design mistakes. But mostly, they're just hilarious.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Design fails are always a laugh (unless, of course, you're the unfortunate soul responsible for them). But beyond providing the internet with an endless supply of entertainment, these spectacular howlers also have a thing or two to teach us. Good design takes skill, patience and effort, so it's hardly surprising that things occasionally go south.

Some of the worst design fails are so blindingly obvious that it's a wonder they made it past the first design meeting, let alone into the real world. But what looks like an unforgivable oversight to the rest of us can be surprisingly easy to miss when you're knee-deep in the design process.

Below we round up 14 ginormous design fails, covering packaging, UI, logo design and UX, all of which offer us all something to learn from. If you'd like to see more on what you should do, see our guide to logo design.

Kicking things off is a hilarious example of ambiguous imagery, from Instituto de Estudos Orientais. Despite being quickly dropped by the university almost a decade ago, it's gone down in history as one of the worst logos of all time. Why? Well, just look at it.

This one's so infamous that one designer, Emanuele Abrate, decided to fix it as part of his 'Worst Logos Ever Redesigned' project in 2020 (above).

The first example in our selection of design fails hit a real bum note, rolling together two huge blunders with unintentionally comical results. The Chinese streaming service iQIY was forced to apologise for this embarrassing Malaysia Day poster.

The design is supposed to read 'Anak Malaysia' (which translates as 'Child of Malaysia') to promote shows available to stream during Malaysia Day 2020. But perhaps in a case of a designer thinking they were too clever to make a mistake, the phrase was stylised to incorporate the crescent and star of the Malaysian flag, resulting in legibility issues that still have us on our bottoms laughing.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Not only does the design make it look like the phrase says 'Anal Malaysia', it also looks like there's a figure squatting down between the two words. Quite how this made it through rounds of approval we're not sure. It leaves us asking whether anyone gave it a look over other than the original designer.

But the potential pitfalls of typography go further than the example above. It's sometimes not enough to only check that your design reads well on a horizontal plane. It's best to check how it looks when turned in other directions too.

The UK Office of Government Commerce existed from 2000 to 2011 with the objective of improving efficiency in public spending. In 2007 it commissioned FHD to design a new logo that would show the body's bold commitment to driving up standards. The problem was that the logo was printed on pens, mouse mats, and all manner of other stationery items that would be viewed from different orientations.

It took the government department's employees mere seconds to notice something that the logo's designers hadn't. Rotated 90 degrees the logo looks rather rude. Incredibly, the OGC decided to stick with the new logo. A spokesperson even quipped: "It is not inappropriate to an organisation that's looking to have a firm grip on Government spend."

Oh my! These days, there’s a graphics department out there that might not have meant to get its lighting decoration go viral... The fact is that #kerning/spacing isn't just a #designer's nitpick! Enjoy a little giggle with this one 🤪🤣 #DesignFail #MarketingFail pic.twitter.com/Uf4qqGG9m8December 6, 2019

Choosing the right font is only the start when it comes to ensuring the typography is right for a design, and that applies to packaging as well as anything else. A little kerning to add space between the 'L' and the 'I' would have been a good idea to prevent misreadings of the product name in the case above.

Always run your design by several people and ask them what they see. Chances are they might have a very different interpretation from those who know what the message is intended to be. And tread very carefully when the word you're designing has any similarity at all to a word you want to avoid.

"As he watches, her body drifts below the water's surface, forever altered. Before he disposes of each victim, he takes a trophy. It's a sign of his power, and a warning…" We're not sure that this novel sounds like the best read out there, but we would never go so far as to demand this fate for the author.

There are many ways to establish text hierarchy – through font, weight, colour, location of text – and sometimes tweaking more than one of these is necessary. In this case, switching from all caps to title case was not enough to prevent an unintended reading of this design, with title placed directly under the author's name. Remember that as a designer, you're not only responsible for how things look but also for their context and how they'll be read.

Perhaps one of the most notorious packaging blunders in history came from this own-brand buttermilk sold by Tesco in Ireland. This shot with crease in the right place went viral and was even picked up by several newspapers after it was taken at a supermarket in Dublin.

Shopper Andy Burdens wrote on Reddit at the time: "They really made a cock and balls of this branding." Tesco changed the design after the media coverage of the blunder. The lesson here? No design is too small or routine to merit making a mockup – designer Oisín Hurst told Creative Bloq he designed the piece on a flat keyline and didn't mock it up, thinking, "it was buttermilk – what could go wrong?"

Logos often combine ideas taken from a brand's name or function. It makes sense then that this bakery in Greece would consider combining a representation of the eponymous 'mama' and the oven in which she presumably bakes her exquisite cakes and breads. The problem is that the position of the flame in the oven makes it look like something else.

It's hard to imagine the designers weren't aware of how it looked and the design may have paid off, the bakery and cafe still proudly using the logo that has taken it to international internet fame.

In general, designers need to be careful with employing the human figure in logos as so many decisions can be misinterpreted, as is demonstrated very well by this 1970s logo for the Catholic Church Archdiocesan Youth Commission. We can perhaps ascribe it to more naive times. And after all, designers can't be expected to predict all future associations that will emerge related to their clients.

The design of marketing campaigns has grown now to encompass a lot more than graphic design. Campaigns often now also include elements of UI and the whole user experience involved in what more interactive initiatives aimed at promoting customer engagement. This kind of marketing has to be well thought through though.

Coca-Cola's custom bottle creator campaign for Pride Month resulted in embarrassment for the company when it turned out the programme would reject certain words from inclusion in the bottles, as well as swear words, the list of banned words seemed to include LGBT, transgender, black lives matter (but not white lives matter) and Palestine.

Presented without comment. cc Venn Diagram enthusiast @LadyFOHF pic.twitter.com/RjYAUjlJaVAugust 14, 2014

This one was a bit of a clanger for the media conglomerate Thomson Reuters. The idea was presumably to suggest that the concepts on the left are the group's core values, but the image looks like a Venn diagram, a type of diagram used to show logical relations between different sets through overlapping curves. Read this way, the graphic appears to show that there is very little overlap between the values on the left and the comparably minute values of Thomson Reuters.

The Washington Post even picked up on the slip and praised the company for its honesty in the ironic headline, "In refreshing change of pace, company admits it's not innovative." The lesson here is to always step back and consider whether design elements you have chosen for purely aesthetic purposes may resemble any other visual language in common use.

Well-heeled travellers will have been a little taken aback when they saw this edition of Where Milan, which is published by Proedi and distributed at many of the city's most exclusive hotels. Placement of the photo cutout layered over the title makes it seem that the publishers are calling into question the reputation of their own cover model.

It's an Italian publication, so the designers could perhaps be forgiven for overlooking an error when working in a foreign language, but if you're aiming at a foreign language market, you need to be able to spot a howler like this.

Another packaging delight, this nutritional supplement from CVS appears to be marketed for Hannibal Lecter. The problem here stems from making assumptions about what the customer knows and following the format of other items in a range of products rather than adapting the design.

For other supplements in this range at US pharmacy chain there's no confusion. The text can read Chewable Vitamin C and no unintentionally comic meaning arises, but with this product it may have been better to clarify in an appropriately sized type that the products inside are tablets avoid skin-crawling misinterpretations.

This is apparently an advert for a graduation day photography service. Getting on the career track maybe? The light at the end of the tract representing a new dawn? Whatever the original intention, there's no getting over the fact that this graduate does not come across as overjoyed about her prospects on the modern job market. In fact, it looks like she's about to end it all by walking straight into an oncoming train.

The colour tones and even the choice of font seem to only back up the impression that the intended message is more about bereavement than celebrating graduation, with the light of the oncoming train perhaps also representing some hope that there may be some better world in the next life. Always consider the mood you want to convey and ensure colours, imagery and type are on message.

It's vital to remember that design is ultimately about clear communication. Just as a writer or speaker needs to choose the perfect words to deliver their message, designers must communicate theirs by selecting the correct visual elements, which convey exactly what they want to say.

Graphic designers producing promotional material and visuals for technical instructions can benefit from having a clear understanding themselves of how the product they're depicting actually works.

When it comes to technology, if the visual material produced for the product doesn't get it right, what chance do the digital immigrants among the customer base have? Just as an aside, we love how retro this design for selfie stick packaging already looks.

These cycling shorts let you fart while riding a bike. It's a neat feature, right? But really who decided it was a good idea to communicate the breathability of these padded shorts by overlaying some gas emerging from the padded rear end?

Sometimes designers have to accept that no designing is needed at all and that conveying a message can be better accomplished by the accompanying text, with no need for added visual clarification.

Looks like I picked a bad day to set this app up pic.twitter.com/R11M7RNU86December 12, 2019

A little UI blooper to finish with. We're not sure why different parts of the world adopted different standard formats for expressing something as straightforward as the date and it is a phenomenon that causes problems for designers everywhere working on UI forms, but this is something that the designer should have foreseen. When you're working with dates or with any other variable content think through all of the possible scenarios that could arise.

Daniel John is Design Editor at Creative Bloq. He reports on the worlds of design, branding and lifestyle tech, and has covered several industry events including Milan Design Week, OFFF Barcelona and Adobe Max in Los Angeles. He has interviewed leaders and designers at brands including Apple, Microsoft and Adobe. Daniel's debut book of short stories and poems was published in 2018, and his comedy newsletter is a Substack Bestseller.

A Cold Wall Names Givenchy and Kenzo alum Joshua Bullen Designer in Residence

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

A Cold Wall is making a fresh start. The label, founded in 2015 by Samuel Ross — and then sold in 2024 — has named Joshua Bullen as its first “designer in residence”. Bullen, 38, whose CV includes Stone Island, Givenchy and Kenzo, will show his first collection for A Cold Wall next January at a presentation in Paris.

Bullen said he plans to build on the progressive identity established by Ross while expanding the brand’s product proposition. “Samuel created something that didn’t exist at the time,” Bullen said of Ross, whose work at A Cold Wall was closely informed by social inclusion and contemporary architecture. “I think respecting that, we really want to push it into something that is primarily a clothing brand. A Cold Wall is unique, and we really have big ambitions for it.”

The appointment marks the latest chapter in the evolution of a brand that emerged as one of the most culturally resonant London labels of the 2010s. A former intern and protégé of Virgil Abloh, Ross established A Cold Wall as a vehicle for examining the intersections of architecture, class, race and contemporary British life through clothing.

Samuel Ross taking a bow at his first Milan runway show.

Ross’s startup attracted an international following and, in 2018, investment from fashion accelerator Tomorrow, which subsequently acquired full ownership when Ross decided to move on to other projects in February 2024. Nine months later, Tomorrow sold the business on to Four Marketing, the London-based distribution and retail group whose largest shareholder is Frasers Group. Ross, meanwhile, has relocated to Northamptonshire in the UK while continuing to develop SR_A, his design studio, where his output spans made-to-order clothing under the Atelier label to industrial design collaborations with partners including Whoop.

“It’s about having a startup mentality with a head start,” said Ben Banks, Four Marketing’s co-founder and CEO, of the opportunity he sees in A Cold Wall. “There is good brand recognition and there’s good social following.” Banks, who mentored Ross through the British Fashion Council’s Newgen program, acknowledged the challenge of turning that recognition into revenue. “As a brand, it was big on cultural and reputational equity, but not so big on overall sales. I think the challenge is to keep those two things relatively aligned.”

Four Marketing is now preparing to expand A Cold Wall’s international reach, particularly in the US, which Banks said already accounts for the largest share of traffic to the brand’s website. The label plans to introduce US-based fulfillment from February 2027 to speed up deliveries and reduce costs for American customers. It is also finalizing arrangements for a US showroom to represent the brand for the FW27 season. Longer term, Banks said the group hopes to open A Cold Wall’s first London store.

Four Marketing has spent nearly three decades building UK businesses for brands including Stone Island and CP Company, while also developing its own acquisitions, including Agent Provocateur in 2017. Banks said he views A Cold Wall as an opportunity to apply that experience to a highly regarded British menswear label whose creative and commercial direction his group can determine for itself.

Looks from the A Cold Wall Fall 2021 collection (left) and Fall 2022 (right).

Bullen’s unusual “designer in residence” title reflects a collaborative approach to the brand’s creative development, and is deliberately open-ended. “There’s no timeline to it: it could last for as long as we want it to last for,” said Bullen, who is also working alongside director and creative consultant Nick Griffiths. “It’s a group thing,” he added. “Neither of us care what our titles are.”

Banks said: “We wanted to bring in a head designer creative that had an alignment of product values without feeling the necessity to impose some sort of completely new code on the brand to stand out.” His ambition, he added, is to create “a progressive selection of garments that also have everyday functionality.” The brand’s name has already been simplified, shedding Ross’s hyphens and asterisk.

For Bullen, the appointment marks a return to fashion design following his departure from Kenzo, where he worked as design director under Nigo. Previously, he was design director of menswear at Givenchy, following more than a decade at Stone Island. Bullen said he is working directly with the brand’s existing product department and added that the forthcoming collection will include tailoring made in London.

The new collection will be presented in Paris during the menswear fashion week in January, alongside a broader cultural program encompassing an exhibition and music. Banks said the objective is to establish an identity that builds on Ross’s original vision without attempting to replicate it. “I think sometimes the biggest mistake that anyone can make is to try and emulate,” he said. “To try and think what somebody else would be thinking, and to mimic it.”

"Direct and unrefined" materials create tactile interior at Jeju restaurant by &Daughters

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Canadian studio &Daughters used basalt, cork, concrete, thatch and wood to design Jeju, a Korean restaurant in Vancouver that features traditional touches in a contemporary interior.

The architect’s starting point for the design was the restaurant’s menu, which includes modern dishes developed by chef Dylan Kim, traditional dishes created by his mother, chef Myungseok “Julie” Suh and dishes that they created together.

“The restaurant exists in the space where these two narratives meet,” &Daughters principal Emma Sims told Dezeen.

“We began to think of tradition and reinterpretation as the foundation for the design, translating this relationship into a physical space through a specific set of people, places, materials and traditions.”

While Jeju’s exterior has a modern steel-mesh facade, the interior juxtaposes wood and concrete for a warmer, more tactile feel.

At the heart of the 44-seat restaurant is a reinterpretation of a traditional thatched house from Jeju Island, South Korea, called a chogajip.

This was constructed using lumber and hand-thatched grass, and creates an artisanal focal point for the space.

Other materials, including basalt, were chosen by &Daughters for Jeju to ensure a natural feel.

Basalt was used for the bar, chef’s table and host stand, while cork cladding helps create a soft feel while adding acoustic dampening.

The studio kept the existing concrete floor but polished it to reveal variations in its colour.

“The palette is quite simple – basalt, cork, raw concrete masonry units, polished concrete, wood and thatch,” Sims said.

“Basalt is one of the most recognisable materials of Jeju Island,” she continued.

“Formed through volcanic activity, it is found throughout the island’s landscape, architecture and traditional stone walls. Beyond that, we were interested in materials that felt direct and unrefined, allowing them to retain their natural character.”

The studio wanted to “introduce elements of tradition, ritual and memory into the interior,” Sims said.

As well as the thatched house, another traditional architectural feature from Jeju, a jeongnang three-pole gate, was also introduced to the interior.

“Historically used as a means of communicating whether a household was home, away or nearby, it offered an alternative to a conventional locked entrance, rooted in trust and shared understanding,” Sims said.

“At Jeju, we incorporated our interpretation into the glazing beside the entrance, where staff lower two poles each day to signal that the restaurant is open and guests are welcome, raising them again at the end of service,” she continued.

“In this way, a traditional architectural element becomes an active part of the restaurant’s daily life.”

Other restaurants recently featured on Dezeen include a brick-clad eatery in London’s Soho area and a bistro in Montreal influenced by Japanese metabolism.

The S&P 500 is an index that tracks the 500 largest publicly traded companies in the United States, weighted by market capitalization. It includes Apple, Microsoft, Amazon, Nvidia, Alphabet (Google), Meta, Berkshire Hathaway, and approximately 493 other companies that collectively represent about 80% of the total U.S. stock market value.

The index is maintained by S&P Dow Jones Indices, which periodically adds and removes companies based on criteria including market cap (must exceed ~$18 billion), profitability, liquidity, and domicile. When a company is added to the S&P 500, every index fund tracking it automatically buys shares — and vice versa when companies are removed.

An S&P 500 index fund is a mutual fund or exchange-traded fund (ETF) that holds all 500 stocks in the same proportions as the index. Instead of picking individual stocks, you buy one fund and instantly own a piece of every major U.S. company. The fund mechanically replicates the index — no stock picking, no market timing, no overpaid fund managers.

The data is overwhelming. Over any 15-year period, approximately 92% of actively managed large-cap funds underperform the S&P 500 after fees, according to the SPIVA scorecard. Over 20 years, that number exceeds 95%. The reasons are simple: active fund managers charge 0.50% to 1.50% in annual fees, they generate taxable trading activity, and they cannot consistently pick winners.

Warren Buffett bet $1 million that an S&P 500 index fund would beat a selection of hedge funds over 10 years. He won decisively — the index fund returned 125.8% vs 36.0% for the hedge fund basket. As Buffett has said: "By periodically investing in an index fund, the know-nothing investor can actually outperform most investment professionals."

The top S&P 500 index funds deliver nearly identical performance because they all track the same index. The differences come down to expense ratio, minimum investment, fund structure (ETF vs mutual fund), and tax efficiency. Here is a detailed comparison of the five most popular options.

The most popular S&P 500 ETF and the default recommendation for most FI seekers. At 0.03% expense ratio, a $500,000 investment costs just $150 per year in fees. VOO is tax-efficient due to the ETF structure and Vanguard's patented heartbeat trade process, which minimizes capital gains distributions. Fractional shares are available at most brokerages.

The lowest-cost S&P 500 fund available at 0.015% — half the cost of VOO. No minimum investment requirement makes it ideal for beginners. As a mutual fund, you can set up automatic investments in exact dollar amounts (e.g., $500/month). FXAIX is an excellent choice if your brokerage is Fidelity.

BlackRock's S&P 500 ETF with a 0.03% expense ratio, matching VOO. Functionally identical performance. Slightly different creation/redemption mechanics than VOO but the practical difference for individual investors is negligible. A strong alternative if you prefer iShares or trade through a brokerage that offers commission-free iShares ETFs.

The oldest and most heavily traded S&P 500 ETF, launched in 1993. SPY is the most liquid ETF in the world, making it the preferred choice for active traders. However, its 0.0945% expense ratio is over three times higher than VOO or IVV. For long-term FI investors, VOO or IVV are better choices. SPY's structure as a unit investment trust also makes it slightly less tax-efficient than VOO.

Schwab's mutual fund offering at 0.02% expense ratio with no minimum investment. Like FXAIX, the mutual fund structure allows automatic dollar-amount investing. An excellent choice for Schwab customers building automated investment plans.

Investing in an S&P 500 fund takes less than 15 minutes:

For retirement accounts (401k, IRA), look for the S&P 500 index fund option within your plan. It may have a different name (e.g., "Large Cap Index Fund" or "Institutional S&P 500") but will track the same index.

The total stock market index (tracked by VTI/VTSAX/FSKAX) holds approximately 3,600 stocks compared to the S&P 500's 500. The additional 3,100 stocks are mid-cap and small-cap companies. Here is how they compare:

Historically, the performance difference between the two is minimal — typically within 0.1-0.3% per year in either direction. The S&P 500 has outperformed in periods when large-cap growth stocks dominate. The total market has outperformed when small and mid-cap stocks lead. Over 20+ year periods, the returns converge closely.

The FI community's honest answer: it does not matter much which you choose. Investing $24,500 per year in VOO or VTI will produce nearly identical outcomes over 20 years. Consistency and contribution amount matter far more than the choice between S&P 500 and total market.

S&P 500 index funds typically yield 1.2% to 1.6% in annual dividends. In a tax-advantaged account (401k, IRA, Roth), dividends compound tax-free. In a taxable account, qualified dividends from S&P 500 funds are taxed at the favorable long-term capital gains rate (0%, 15%, or 20% depending on income).

ETFs (VOO, IVV) are generally more tax-efficient than mutual funds (FXAIX, SWPPX) in taxable accounts because ETFs rarely distribute capital gains. Vanguard's mutual funds are an exception — their patented ETF share class structure gives Vanguard mutual funds ETF-like tax efficiency. For taxable brokerage accounts, VOO or VFIAX (Vanguard's mutual fund version) are the most tax-efficient choices.

Free calculators, a personalized FI roadmap, and a community of 25K+ on the same journey.

The most common mistake with S&P 500 index funds is waiting for the "right time" to invest. Research from Vanguard shows that investing a lump sum immediately outperforms dollar-cost averaging about two-thirds of the time, because markets go up more often than they go down. Missing just the 10 best trading days over a 20-year period can cut your returns nearly in half. If you have money to invest, the data overwhelmingly says to invest it now — not next week, not after the next pullback, not when it "feels right."

Open an account at Vanguard (for VOO/VFIAX), Fidelity (for FXAIX), or Schwab (for SWPPX). All three offer commission-free trading on their own index funds, excellent customer service, and robust retirement account options. You can also buy any of these funds at most brokerages.

ETFs (VOO, IVV) trade like stocks at real-time prices and are more tax-efficient in taxable accounts. Mutual funds (FXAIX, SWPPX) allow automatic dollar-amount investing and are better for set-it-and-forget-it contribution schedules. For retirement accounts, either works equally well.

Transfer cash to your brokerage account and buy your chosen fund. Start with whatever you have — there is no minimum too small. FXAIX and SWPPX have $0 minimums, and VOO can be purchased in fractional shares at most brokerages for as little as $1.

Automate monthly or per-paycheck contributions. This removes emotion from the process and ensures consistent dollar-cost averaging. Most brokerages let you schedule recurring purchases of mutual funds. For ETFs, some brokerages offer automatic ETF purchases as well.

If you hold other asset classes (bonds, international), rebalance once per year to maintain your target allocation. Otherwise, do nothing — do not check daily prices, do not sell during downturns, and do not chase performance. The S&P 500 has recovered from every crash in history. Time in the market beats timing the market.

Fidelity 500 Index Fund (FXAIX) has the lowest expense ratio at 0.015%. Schwab S&P 500 Index Fund (SWPPX) is next at 0.02%. VOO and IVV tie at 0.03%. All four are excellent — the fee difference between FXAIX (0.015%) and VOO (0.03%) on a $500,000 portfolio is just $75 per year. Choose the fund offered by your preferred brokerage.

VOO is better for long-term investors. It charges 0.03% vs SPY's 0.0945%, saving you about $325 per year on a $500,000 portfolio. VOO is also more tax-efficient due to its ETF share class structure. SPY's advantage is liquidity for active trading, which long-term index investors do not need. For buy-and-hold FI investing, VOO is the clear winner.

Either is an excellent choice. The S&P 500 covers approximately 80% of the U.S. stock market by value. A total market fund adds mid-cap and small-cap stocks for fuller diversification. Over long periods, performance is nearly identical — typically within 0.1-0.3% per year. The FI community consensus is that the best fund is the one you will consistently invest in without overthinking the decision.

As little as $1. FXAIX and SWPPX have zero minimum investments. VOO and IVV can be purchased as fractional shares at most brokerages. There is no minimum too small — starting with $50 or $100 per month and increasing contributions as your income grows is exactly how most people on the FI path begin.

The S&P 500 has returned approximately 10.3% per year on average since 1957 (including dividends). Adjusted for inflation, the real return is approximately 7.0% per year. These are long-term averages — individual years range from -38% (2008) to +38% (1995). The key is staying invested through the down years to capture the recovery gains that follow.

S&P 500 index funds carry market risk — they can lose significant value in the short term (the index dropped 34% in early 2020 and 38% in 2008). However, the S&P 500 has recovered from every decline in its history and has never lost money over any 20-year period. For investors with a 10+ year time horizon, the risk of not investing (missing out on 10% average annual returns) is far greater than the risk of temporary market declines.

S&P 500 index funds are the single most important building block for long-term wealth. With expense ratios as low as 0.015%, you keep virtually all of your returns while owning a piece of the 500 largest U.S. companies. VOO, FXAIX, IVV, and SWPPX all deliver near-identical performance — choose the fund offered by your preferred brokerage and automate your contributions. The FI community has proven through thousands of real-world examples that consistent investing in a low-cost S&P 500 index fund, held for decades without panic selling, is the most reliable path to building wealth and reaching financial independence.

ECB Links Europe’s T+1 Securities Transition to a Broader Settlement Overhaul

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: European Central Bank officials have discussed the planned move to T+1 securities settlement in October 2027 alongside longer-term work on tokenized settlement infrastructure. A future regulatory settlement date should not be confused with a finished network rollout.

The deadline matters most to firms that process transactions across several jurisdictions. They have to harmonise instructions, reconcile holdings and manage currencies while the clock gets shorter. Blockchain advocates should explain specifically which tasks become easier and which stay with regulated intermediaries.

European Central Bank officials have discussed the planned move to T+1 securities settlement in October 2027 alongside longer-term work on tokenized settlement infrastructure. A speech published October 29 outlined how shortening the conventional settlement cycle and experimenting with new technology fit into wider financial-market modernisation. The two projects are related strategic topics, not a statement that blockchain technology will automatically replace Europe’s existing settlement systems next year. The dated original disclosure is the starting point for the figures and product scope discussed here.

T+1 means most eligible securities trades settle one business day after the trade date, compared with a longer conventional cycle. Shorter cycles can reduce outstanding exposures and certain capital needs, but they compress the time available to resolve breaks, fund positions and coordinate across time zones. Tokenization addresses a different design question: how ownership records and money can move using new technical infrastructure.

The connection is operational discipline. A market trying to settle sooner needs robust automation, accurate records and predictable funding. Tokenized assets may eventually support more tightly connected workflows, but they are not necessary to achieve the near-term T+1 timetable. Existing post-trade institutions and banks still have crucial responsibilities.

A tokenized instrument must connect technical records with enforceable financial rights. Trading software can move a token quickly, but an issuer, custodian, transfer agent or settlement bank may still have to recognise the result. This is why tokenization announcements need to explain which part of the lifecycle is changing, not merely advertise that a blockchain is involved.

This development also connects to two subjects our newsroom has been following: DTCC’s tokenized securities infrastructure rollout and tokenized fund redemptions tested over a weekend. They explain related market structure and should not be mistaken for evidence that every new proposal is already live.

A future regulatory settlement date should not be confused with a finished network rollout. Different products and markets can have exclusions or distinct implementation rules. Nor should a policy speech be read as authorising every private tokenized security to circulate without existing investor-protection requirements.

Real settlement infrastructure is judged by whether it works on bad days, not just during presentations. Reconciliation, asset eligibility, funding, corporate actions and recovery from errors remain central. Continuous technical operation can improve coordination, yet it does not create market liquidity or remove the obligations of regulated participants.

Watch for detailed industry testing, bank funding arrangements and official updates on both T+1 readiness and central-bank-money experiments. The important measure is whether settlement failures and costs fall without shifting uncontrolled risk to participants. Europe’s timeline creates a concrete deadline, while the tokenization debate remains a longer-term architecture choice.

China outlines plans for moonshot it hopes to land by 2030

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

We have go for launch, and the ethernet and Wi-Fi standards that will make it happen

China’s crewed space program has outlined plans for a moon landing it hopes to achieve by the year 2030.

Deputy chief designer Wang Xiang explained the plans last weekend at an event called the Seventh Manned Space Symposium. Chinese state media has posted video of Wang discussing plans to reach Luna by launching two Long March 10 rockets.

One will carry a lander named “Lanyue” and the other will hoist a crew capsule called “Mengzhou.” The two craft will meet in orbit around the Moon, where two of the three Taikonauts aboard Mengzhou will transfer to Lanyue and descend to set boot on regolith.

In the video, Wang said China plans near-Earth orbit test missions for both vehicles in coming years, followed by an uncrewed visit to the Moon. If all goes well, China hopes to attempt a crewed landing by 2030.

The Seventh Manned Space Symposium marked the 70th anniversary of the founding of China's space program and was also pitched as a moment for articulation of the nation’s ambitions in space under its 15th five-year-plan, Beijing’s blueprint for the years 2026 to 2030.

China called off its last moonshot in August, when authorities decided the Chang’e 7 robotic ice hunting mission “failed to meet launch criteria” just one day before its planned launch. The orbit required to reach the South Lunar Pole for that mission isn’t always easily accessible, so China plans to try again next year.

When China tries to get Chang’e 7, Lanyue, and Mengzhou flying, it will at least have some new standards to apply to their operations after the China Manned Space Engineering Office last week published seventeen of them. One of the standards defines the architecture for Ethernet and Wi-Fi networks on China’s space station and other orbiters. Another governs signage used in orbit, in the hope that a standard means Taikonauts won’t make mistakes. A couple outline procedures for extravehicular activities, and use of a robotic arm to assist Taikonauts on such missions. Others explain the materials designers can choose from when building spacecraft. ®

Maine Senator Susan Collins Has Reportedly Conceded Race to Democratic Challenger Troy Jackson

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

One of the most closely watched states during the run-up to the 2026 midterm elections has been Maine, where incumbent US senator Susan Collins, a Republican, faced a significant threat from upstart candidate Graham Platner. Allegations of misconduct toppled the Democrat’s campaign, but his replacement, former logger and state senator Troy Jackson, has had a strong showing in the polls, and posed the first actual competition Collins has faced in nearly three decades. A little more than three hours after Maine's poll closure at 8 p.m. ET, Collins reportedly called Jackson to concede the race, even as most news outlets were reporting that the race was still ongoing.

Susan Collins was running for a sixth term, and was the only Republican representing a state President Donald Trump lost in 2024. As votes rolled in on Tuesday and into Wednesday morning, Jackson's lead continued to grow. According to outlets, including Maine broadcast station WMTW, Collins called Jackson shortly after midnight to congratulate him for his win. In an address to supporters early Wednesday, an ebullient Jackson celebrated his win. At the time of publication, the New York Times was forecasting that “Jackson is almost certain to win," and NBC had projected him the winner as well.

“20 minutes or so I spoke to Senator Collins and I thanked her for her service to Maine,” Jackson said. “I wished her and her family well.”

“To every Mainer who voted for her or supported her, you can count on me to be your senator now.”

Elsewhere in Maine, Democrats kept the governor’s office blue, as term-limited governor Janet Mills will be replaced by Democrat Hannah Pingree, the Associated Press said when they called the race Tuesday night. She bested Republican candidate Bobby Charles, according to the AP.

Maine only has two congressional districts, both of which are up for election every two years. The state’s First Congressional District is known as a Democratic stronghold, with Hannah Pingree’s mother, Chellie Pingree, expected to retain the seat she’s held since 2009. Its Second District is considered a battleground region, with former state secretary of state Matthew Dunlap, a Democrat, still locked in a tight race against Republican former governor Paul LePage as of publication time.

Cristian Mungiu’s Cinematographer Tudor Vladimir Panduru Breaks Down Long Takes on ‘Fjord,’ ‘R.M.N.’ at Jakarta World Cinema Masterclass

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Known for his long-time collaboration with Romanian auteur Cristian Mungiu, cinematographer Tudor Vladimir Panduru shared his experience making “Graduation,” “R.M.N.,” “Fjord” and Iraqi director Hasan Hadi’s “The President’s Cake” during his masterclass at Jakarta World Cinema.

Benh Zeitlin, the Oscar-nominated director of “Beasts of the Southern Wild,” said he was moderating the session because “we hope to work together someday.” He praised Panduru as the kind of cinematographer who could “bring a dimension to your work that is beyond your personal imagination.” Related Stories ‘Father’s House,’ Starring David Dencik and Trine-Dyrholm, Picked Up by DR Sales Ahead of Tallinn Competition

The masterclass traced Panduru’s career chronologically, starting with the screening of one indoor and one outdoor scene from “Graduation.” Panduru trained under Oleg Mutu, Mungiu’s longtime cinematographer on films including “4 Months, 3 Weeks and 2 Days,” and was introduced to the Romanian director while still a trainee. “Graduation” was Panduru’s second film as a cinematographer, but it was also the one that made his name internationally.

Panduru said the camera in Mungiu’s films is meant to stay low-profile and avoid steering the audience toward certain things, because viewers are “smart enough to understand what’s going on there.”

“With Cristian Mungiu’s work, he usually shoots plan-séquence, one scene per shot. There is no editing, no cuts. From a cinematographer’s point of view, things can be quite difficult because we end up shooting a lot of takes, and the performance comes as somehow the most important thing on set,” said Panduru. “We end up shooting 20, 30, 40 takes sometimes to reach the best outcome.”

The grueling repetition of long takes, however, was not as dreary as one might imagine, as Mungiu is able to keep everyone fresh throughout. Panduru also pointed out that the responsibility to stay fresh lies with each individual, since shooting is about living the moment with everyone on set.

“It’s also a matter of trust. When you get to work in this kind of style, everybody gives a bit of himself into that process, so there’s a moment of synchronicity,” he said. “We don’t shoot 30 takes because the takes are not good. [The one that works] needs to have all the elements get together so you have the best outcome of everything.”

Zeitlin said Mungiu’s films value “directness and truthfulness” and criticize “hypocrisy and dishonesty.” Panduru agreed, saying Romanian cinema has often been rooted in social issues and that what Mungiu addresses is important. “I’m just trying to help him tell that,” he said.

On his choice of projects, Panduru said artists should collaborate based on shared values, and that he has been lucky enough to choose the subjects that drew him. He ruled out making an action film, “or maybe only if it’s something that has a social drama behind. Everything relates to affinities.”

During the masterclass, several of Panduru’s defining scenes were screened, each followed by a technical breakdown of how it was shot: the 20-minute take of more than 100 people gathered in the town hall in “R.M.N.,” the long shot alongside a sailing boat on the water in “The President’s Cake,” and two scenes from Mungiu’s latest film, “Fjord.”

The use of light in “R.M.N.” led the discussion, with Panduru revealing that the daytime scene was in fact shot at night. “You’re basically using the focus pulling as a way to edit and also reframe in the same shot without having the camera moving at all,” he said of the hardest part, keeping more than 100 people in focus in a single frame.

Zeitlin said that when he saw the clip from “The President’s Cake,” he realized the same person had shot the Mungiu film. “I was like, I have to talk to the cinematographer,” he said, adding that the film’s aesthetic was distinctly different from Mungiu’s.

Panduru said the film was a children’s fairytale that naturally called for a different visual language. The natural landscape inspired him to build scenes more creatively, freeing him to use running jib arms and wheels, techniques he went on to use again on “Fjord.”

The first clip from “Fjord,” a family gathering that appeared to be shot at “magic hour,” the brief window just before sunset, turned the discussion back to light. Panduru explained how he blocked the natural light from outside to buy more time for the performances.

The masterclass ended with another long take from “Fjord,” in which the camera follows star Renate Reinsve from searching on the ground to jumping into the sea.

“It was really important to have the entire arc of the scene of the emotional trajectory of the characters, [so] it was really important for Cristian not to cut in this moment,” said Panduru.

The cinematographer, who jumped into the freezing sea alongside Reinsve, said he thought that would be the most straightforward way of shooting it, but that the hardest part was synchronizing with the actor mid-jump. “And the second, while I was doing [it], if I lost the camera in the moment of the jump –” he said, as the audience laughed. “I had a really great team of grips who were supporting me in the water and also outside the water.”

Ethereum dev targets 2028 for quantum resistance, a year early

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Ethereum researcher Justin Drake said it is their “hope” that Ethereum will achieve its post-quantum and post-AI transition by December 2028.

Ethereum Foundation researcher Justin Drake is targeting December 2028 to complete Ethereum’s post-quantum and post-AI transition, a year earlier than the foundation’s previously announced quantum-resistance deadline.

Speaking at Devcon 8 in Mumbai on Tuesday, Drake outlined the accelerated timeline. “Our hope is that by December 2028 we’ll be able to wrap up the full post-quantum and post-AI transition,” Drake said, adding that developers have 24 months to deliver three additional network upgrades.

The Ethereum Foundation’s September roadmap established December 2029 as its deadline for achieving quantum resistance across Ethereum’s execution, consensus and data layers.

Drake’s remarks signal a more ambitious timeline but do not formally replace the foundation’s existing roadmap, which is scheduled for reassessment in January 2027.

In August, Drake outlined plans for production-ready leanVM technology in 2027, followed by post-quantum deployments across Ethereum’s three layers in 2028.

The timeline has drawn scrutiny from other blockchain developers. In October, Starknet’s community backed research into becoming an independent post-quantum layer-1 network.

StarkWare CEO Eli Ben-Sasson previously told Cointelegraph that Ethereum’s 2029 target was “too late,” warning that deadlines could slip.

Prime Video's Muhammad Ali Biopic 'The Greatest' Doesn't Do the Legend Justice | Review

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

One of the most anticipated shows coming to Prime Video is The Greatest from executive producer Michael B. Jordan, an official biopic about the former undisputed heavyweight champion of the world, Muhammad Ali. A decade after his death at the age of 74, his career, charisma, and advocacy still fascinate generations of fans. Sports dramas have provided numerous hits on streaming, with docuseries like FX's Welcome to Wrexham and Netflix's Drive to Survive racing up the charts alongside fictional offerings like Apple TV's Ted Lasso. Can Prime Video create another hit with arguably the most compelling life story in sports history?

The Prime Video series portrays the life of Ali, then known as Cassius Clay (Jaalen Best), from his breakthrough fight at the Rome Olympics in 1960 to his pivotal 1964 championship bout with Sonny Liston (Amin Joseph). Fighting through a tempestuous relationship with his father, Cassius Sr. (Omari Hardwick), racial prejudice, and talented competition, he forges a path that makes himone of the most important athletes of the 20th century. The narrative is framed around Ali's relationship with his family, as well as his growing connection with civil rights leader Malcolm X (Michael Ealy).

The series is produced by Amazon MGM Studios in collaboration with Muhammad Ali’s estate, with his widow Lonnie serving as executive producer alongside Oscar winner Jordan, and was created by Ben Watkins, the showrunner of the Prime Video series Cross. It’s the first fully authorized scripted television series based on the champion’s life, and while it only focuses on the early years of his career, it’s reasonable to assume future seasons would feature him at the height of his fame.

What The Greatest does well is highlight the opponents Ali faced outside the ring. Set in the racially segregated South, the young boxer is heralded by white dignitaries for his achievements at the Olympics, but reminded through several hurtful remarks and gestures that he is still viewed as inferior by the people of his hometown. The young man, initially wearing his gold medal proudly, is driven to despair as he realizes the bigotry that still lies in plain sight. It’s a powerful reminder of what he faced, even if the point is hammered home indelicately.

One interesting twist is the use of contemporary music in many scenes, presumably due to the show being partly produced by Jay-Z’s Roc Nation. The narrative device yields mixed results, working well when looking at the boxing side of his life but occasionally feeling out of place elsewhere. The boxing footage is, as you would hope, well-choreographed and dramatic, creating a stage on which Ali would become its brightest star.

Many biopics about great creatives have run into the problem of their subject’s life not being as exciting as their work. The Greatest has the opposite issue — Ali’s life was filled with legendary moments, captured on camera at the time and examined by some of the most talented filmmakers of all time. Michael Mann directed the 2001 film Ali with Will Smith, made with Ali’s blessing, while documentaries like When We Were Kings and miniseries Muhammad Ali: The Whole Story brought archive footage to life. Ali even starred in his own biopic, also called The Greatest, in 1977.

In short, there have been hours of TV and cinema devoted to his story, and The Greatest suffers by comparison. The script borders on melodrama, delivering a surface-level retelling of events but never quite letting the viewer in on the inner workings of Ali’s or his family's minds. Moments that should have been jaw-dropping drama, such as the refusal of service at a restaurant that leads to Ali throwing his gold medal in the river, feel clinical and devoid of tension. The episodes plod along a familiar biopic path, which feels like a crime when talking about a story as unique as this.

It’s a thankless task to play one of sports history's most iconic figures, particularly one known for being such a showman. Jaalen Best does a solid job, not least through his physical transformation, mimicking Ali’s cadence and bravado while also portraying the shy young man behind the scenes. What prevents it from being a truly remarkable performance is the shallow material that breaks down Ali's life into a series of confrontations and montages rather than allowing a peek into the mentality of a boxing great.

Elsewhere in the cast, Hardwick has some compelling moments as a man struggling to reconcile with his son achieving the greatness he wanted, while Quentin Tarantino collaborator Dana Gourrier brings heart to the story as Ali’s mother, Odessa “Bird” Clay. Joseph, best known for the hit show Snowfall, is impressive as Sonny Liston, depicting the lesser-known story of his path to glory as well as his tender romance with his wife, Geraldine (Erica Tazel). Ealy and Giovanni Ribisi make notable appearances as Malcolm X and trainer Angelo Dundee, respectively, but their star power can’t elevate something that should have you on the edge of your seat.

While authorized biopics have an air of authenticity, they can also lead to a project that plays things a little too safe. The Greatest never quite captures the excitement of the man it portrays, nor does the behind-the-scenes drama feel like much more than a detailed Wikipedia entry. Despite some interesting flourishes, what should have been a gripping tale of genius turns into a by-the-numbers biopic that has been told much better elsewhere.

The Greatest premieres November 4 on Prime Video.

A missed opportunity to go deeper into the life of a sports legend.

The Greatest is an intimate exploration of the legendary life of Muhammad Ali, examining his victories and defeats within the boxing ring and his personal journey outside the spotlight as a man, husband, father, brother, and son.

Sports Leagues Can’t Stop Putting Ad Revenue Ahead Of Fan And Player Interests

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

I recently rewatched the famous Mets vs. Red Sox 1986 World Series game 6 broadcast to celebrate its 40th anniversary.

If you’re not familiar, perhaps you recall the “Curb Your Enthusiasm” episode that plays on former Red Sox first baseman Bill Buckner’s inability to field a ball.

As a Mets fan who wasn’t alive in 1986 and has never seen a World Series victory, this particular game is part of the lore I grew up with. But until this rewatch, I had never been so keenly struck by how much the fan experience of sports has changed.

The 1986 World Series broadcast obviously had no digitally superimposed brand banners behind home plate and along the field walls; nor was the pitcher’s mound virtually branded with a sponsor logo. The broadcast also runs for tens of seconds at a time with just fan noise filling the air.

Today, the silence would be occupied by announcers doing sponsor integrations, picture-in-picture-style ads overlaid on the live broadcast, pop-up marketing along the lower bar of the TV, maybe even an entire commercial break inserted into the action.

There is a powerful undercurrent of disaffection and outright anger from the American populace toward US sporting institutions right now. Often, that manifests as frustration over how complicated and expensive it is to find and watch a game, or private equity ownership of kids’ sports leagues and equipment that drives up the cost to play.

Against this backdrop, griping about advertising taking over sports almost feels quaint, like a throwback to a less-commercialized era.

But this isn’t just about logos cluttering the playing field. Sports leagues and teams are making decisions that sacrifice player safety and the enjoyment of their own fans in various bids for marketing revenue.

The biggest example of sporting greed in recent memory has to be the World Cup “hydration breaks” introduced this year.

Let’s call these what they were: 3-minute-long commercial breaks inserted into the first and second halves of each game.

FIFA proposed World Cup hydration breaks ostensibly on player welfare grounds, since high temperatures were forecasted for some of the games. Never mind that players and coaches blasted the breaks for messing with the usual flow of a soccer game.

During the World Cup, FIFA denied that it was profiting from the breaks. But post-tournament, FIFA has been unabashedly bragging about the breaks’ successful sponsorship haul. FIFA will now require them as part of future World Cup broadcast deals, regardless of whether the host nation’s weather calls for water breaks.

The shameless cash grab of hydration breaks is just one example of how pro soccer leagues prioritize sponsor interests. And the ploy of calling it player welfare is an affront, considering there are real player safety issues that clash with sponsor interests. And the leagues always defer to ad revenue.

Consider those waist-high walls of advertisements – sometimes made of brick or concrete but increasingly composed of LED screens – which are now omni-present around pro soccer fields.

Having just played DIII NCAA soccer, I am shocked by how closely hedged pro soccer fields are by these walls of ads. Players run full-tilt just feet away from these things. And they’re wearing metal-tipped cleats, so any jump over or atop the wall is fraught with accidental danger.

“Players must get hurt all the time,” I’ve often thought. Turns out, they do.

British midfielder Jordan Henderson was forced out of the World Cup with a broken wrist that needed surgery after toppling over a sideline ad board. The Paraguayan men’s team coach called for a reassessment of the practice when his striker was hurt crashing into an ad screen during a game with Australia. Togo international player Samuel Asamoah had his neck broken in a collision with an ad board a year ago. (He’s alive but partially paralyzed.) That incident came not long after Billy Vigar, a young British player coming up with the Chichester City club, died in a similar headfirst tumble.

For years now, players and lower-level leagues without major broadcast sponsor demands have called for reviews of what’s dubbed “pitch perimeter safety.”

The fix is simple. Just move the boards back a little bit. There’s often a huge expanse of space available between the ads and the stadium seats.

But the rationale for having ads hug the perimeter of the field is because that’s how you create viewable inventory for TV. Sponsors are paying for constant on-screen presence, and that’s not feasible if the screens are backed up to a safe distance.

And the problem goes well beyond international fútbol.

The US Open tennis tournament this year made the marketing decision to give many social media creator-influencer types free tickets. Which sparked backlash from players and fans.

The stadiums were full of influencers doing their livestreams and vlogs, not bothering to watch the tennis. And the niceties of tennis etiquette (as in, quieting down during play) were largely lost on the creator crowd. At least one player even stopped midmatch to complain about the smell of weed wafting from the audience (lol).

Whether it’s social media tweenagers muddying the experience of the US Open or a pointless stoppage of World Cup play brought to you by Powerade, it can feel like the wearisome beat of progress or modernity that’s changing these games.

But, actually, the only thing that’s inevitable is sports leagues putting advertisers’ interests above that of fans and players.

Look at the NFL, which is already the most lucrative and heavily saturated sport when it comes to advertising, since it’s a game with plenty of natural stoppages and downtime. But even the NFL can’t resist the temptation to cram in more ads any more than FIFA.

The NFL RedZone channel, created in 2009, for years had the tagline “seven hours of commercial-free football.” But commercials arrived in 2024, and now the show has some 16 ad breaks per Sunday. Relatedly, ESPN acquired the NFL Network and distribution rights to RedZone last year.

And none of the above even approaches the most insidious aspect of modern sports marketing: partnerships with sportsbook apps and prediction markets.

All of the big five US sports leagues – the NFL, NBA, MLB, NHL and MLS (sure, we’ll go ahead and count MLS) – have partnerships with gambling companies and feature them prominently during TV broadcasts. Top players endorse the gambling apps, and the leagues provide live, in-broadcast marketing integrations for betting lines, prop bets and insights from DraftKings, Fanatics, FanDuel, et al.

And yet, these five leagues recently formed a new lobbying coalition to advocate for state laws cracking down on sportsbook app users who threaten or harass players. Call it a stopgap against the inevitability that some fans are going to lose their shirts betting on the games and blame the players.

No wonder sports gambling marketing integrations are mostly loathed by players (presumably aside from those who are paid endorsers). Athletes have to weigh the chance to be the next generation’s childhood hero against being constantly lambasted by young or middle-aged men who’ve lost their paychecks to an app.

The leagues would prefer to offload the issue to state legislatures. But what if the problem is coming from inside their own house?

It is the leagues and their broadcast partners that time and again have failed to uphold standards of quality and, frankly, decency when marketing revenue is in play.

Some of the biggest Super Bowl advertisers of the past few years have been the sportsbooks themselves. DraftKings and Fanatics advertised during this year’s Big Game, while FanDuel ramped up its marketing in the lead-up to the event.

But before the sportsbook apps, there were back-to-back years when the Super Bowl was taken over by Temu – which, not to put too fine a point on it, is a malicious Chinese government infosec data-capture vehicle that also sells shoddy merchandise.  The NFL and CBS let that company buy six Super Bowl ads in 2024.

Before Temu, multiple crypto exchanges, including the now-infamous pyramid scheme FTX, were big NFL and Super Bowl sponsors.

That list seriously calls into question the judgement of the NFL and its Super Bowl broadcasters (primarily NBC or Fox, depending on the year).

Is there seriously no interest in live sports from quality advertisers? Or are more reputable sponsors just being outbid by brands like FTX, Temu and DraftKings?

More worryingly still, do the NFL, CBS, Fox, NBC and the rest no longer know the difference between good and bad brand associations?

The answer can be “Yes all around,” by the way.

But while the leagues might be missing the picture, some of their brand partners aren’t.

Despite the smashing ad monetization success of this year’s World Cup from FIFA’s perspective, its biggest brand sponsors were nonplussed. McDonald’s is pulling its 32-year World Cup sponsorship, which “underperformed our expectations,” CFO Ian Borden told investors during a quarterly earnings report last week.

And other publicly traded brands like Adidas, Visa, Hershey and Coca-Cola are all hard-pressed to justify their sports marketing investments right now.

As those well-known global consumer brands reconsider their sports budgets, they send sports leagues into the open arms of sportsbooks, GLP-1s, crypto exchanges and other forms of cynical advertising.

As always, the losers are the fans and the players.

People Inc.’s referral traffic from Google search is down 70% over the past two years, and by 26% in Q3 alone. But its digital revenue is still growing, albeit slowly.

On Tuesday, Chord, a six-year-old commerce tech startup, acquired Zenlytic, an AI startup with an anthropomorphized data analyst agent named “Zoë,” for $5 million.

If you ask the executive leadership of Affle and Perion why the two companies are merging, the answer you’ll get is “synergies.”

A combo of Tubi, sports and political ads powered Fox’s growth this last quarter, even as Fox News lost viewers and the Roku deal awaits DOJ clearance.

It’s fall, and M&A is in the air. Two acquisitions happened this week: Stagwell bought Freestar, and DoorDash bought Instacart.

Starting in Q4, Reddit will test logged-in-only mode to protect its user engagement, improve ad targeting and keep AI from scraping its content.

A Parachute for Your ADHD Medication Crash

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Most people taking ADHD medication experience an afternoon crash. Here, ADDitude readers reveal effective strategies for a softer landing.

It’s 4 p.m. and your brain is suddenly caught in a riptide. You feel like a swimmer fighting a strong current. The focus, priorities, and momentum that have propelled you forward until now are submerged under a layer of brain fog that rolls in around the same time each day.

This phenomenon is called the ADHD medication crash, and it happens to two-thirds of ADDitude readers who take ADHD medication. When medication levels drop rapidly in the bloodstream, this can result in fatigue, slowed processing speed, and inertia.

According to 3,500 ADDitude survey responses, 57 to 78 percent of stimulant medication users experience a regular crash, with dextroamphetamine and Ritalin LA users reporting the highest prevalence. Even people taking the most popular stimulant medications reported crash prevalence rates of near or above 60 percent. (Users’ experiences with the most popular stimulant medications are shown in the chart below.) The lowest crash prevalence rates were reported by people taking the non-stimulant medications Atomoxetine/Strattera (40%) and guanfacine (52.9%), suggesting that medication crashes are less common with non-stimulant ADHD treatments.

The most common signs of a medication crash, according to ADDitude readers who experience them, include:

[Get This Free Download: ADHD Treatment Guide for Adults]

The odds of a medication crash increase when an individual is experiencing stress, inadequate sleep, poor diet, and lack of exercise, according to survey respondents. Almost two-thirds of women who experience crashes said their menstrual cycle impacts the crash symptom severity. Readers did not cite medication interactions or caffeine as significant contributors.

The ability to manage household responsibilities is “often” or “always” impaired by medication crashes for 70 percent of survey respondents. Half of crashers say work responsibilities “often” or “always” suffer, and 43 percent say social and family interactions suffer.

Ben Shapiro Says 2026 Midterms Were “A Wipeout for Republicans”: “The President Was Kind of on the Ballot”

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The conservative commentator noted Tuesday night, before most of the major race results were called, that "this not an evening to remember."

Ben Shapiro reacted to the early results of the 2026 midterms during an appearance on the All-In podcast Tuesday night, noting that he thinks early tallies indicated that “the president was kind of on the ballot.”

“This not an evening to remember,” he said on the podcast, which streamed from 8 p.m. ET to 10 p.m. ET on Tuesday night, before some of the major race results were called. “I think that, honestly, the president said going in that the election was going to be a referendum on him, act like he’s on the ballot, and I think that, you know, the president was kind of on the ballot.” Related Stories Midterms 2026 Xavier Becerra Defeats Steve Hilton to Become California's First Latino Governor in 150 Years Midterms 2026 Texas Turns Blue: How James Talarico, Gina Hinojosa Broke Dems' 36-Year Losing Streak

Shapiro argued “if this were just the case of a few outstandingly bad Republican candidates losing, like Paxton in Texas, for example, or if it were just a matter of an outstanding Democrat candidate winning somewhere, like Josh Turek in Iowa, … but the fact that South Carolina is going, you basically have an empty suit in South Carolina losing.”

Texas had history-making results, as the state elected James Talarico as defeated Ken Paxton, becoming the first Democrat elected as a senator in 38 years; Gina Hinojosa also beat Greg Abbott to become the first Democratic governor of Texas in 36 years.

As for South Carolina, Annie Andrews became the state’s first Democrat elected to the Senate in over 20 years. She also became the first woman from South Carolina to be elected to the Senate.

“Or the fact that Florida stayed red, but very, very, very closely. I mean, that’s my home state so I know that state very well, Angie Nixon is a full-scale nutcase, and Angie Nixon is going to lose that senatorial race by about two points,” he added. (Democrat Nixon was defeated by Republican Ashley Moody in Florida’s U.S. Senator race.)

“It’s a wipeout for Republicans,” Shapiro asserted. “There’s no way to sugarcoat that. And again, if you’re looking at it across the map, that bodes pretty ill for 2028.”

DoorDash’s Instacart Deal: What A Successful Integration Will Mean For Retailers

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Running a local business is already a full-time job. Becoming an expert in every advertising platform shouldn’t become a second one.

So will DoorDash’s $17 billion acquisition of Instacart make local retailers’ jobs any easier?

It can, if the combined company makes its tools practical for businesses that can’t hire a highly specialized marketing team.

Streamlining operations for retail media advertisers is one of the biggest untapped opportunities in ad tech. Which is why consolidation is coming to local commerce.

Recently, DoorDash has added value by partnering and buying rather than building everything itself.

Its 2025 acquisition of Symbiosys, valued at $175 million, extended its advertising capabilities into search, social and display. Instacart would bring deeper grocery expertise, relationships with more than 2,200 retail banners and technology built for the complexity of running a grocery business. Many grocers already use that technology to run their own sites and retail media networks.

For merchants, the question is what they can realistically manage.

Commerce media asks two different things of grocers. Every retailer needs to advertise its own business, reaching shoppers on search, social and delivery apps. Some also run media networks for CPG advertisers, which means selling ad inventory, extending it off-site and demonstrating incrementality to brands that expect closed-loop measurement.

Not every local or midsize grocer needs to launch a network. But whichever role a retailer takes on, it has to do it all on slim grocery margins.

Either way, a retailer should be able to reach more customers without having to become an expert in every advertising platform. That’s the promise for local businesses in the DoorDash/Instacart deal: access to more sophisticated marketing and commerce capabilities without having to assemble and manage every piece. The companies say more grocers could use Instacart’s technology to run their own websites and retail media networks, alongside both companies’ advertising and AI tools.

DoorDash’s moves on the consumer side – particularly its app-bundling strategy – also impact its network’s reach and its usefulness for local grocers and advertisers.

The person ordering dinner and the person buying groceries are often the same person, working with the same household budget. A bundle has to earn its place in that budget.

DoorDash has partnered its way into being more useful to consumers. Since late 2024, US DashPass members who link a Lyft account get 5% off on-demand rides and 10% off up to four scheduled airport rides a month. DoorDash didn’t have to build a rideshare business to offer it.

The Instacart deal applies the same idea to groceries. After closing, households that pay for both DashPass and Instacart+ would pay a single membership fee, which the companies estimate would save members $100 million a year combined.

A membership that covers dinner, groceries and the ride home is easier to justify keeping.

Two adoption questions are converging. For consumers: What makes the service worth trying and the membership worth keeping? For local businesses: What makes these tools practical to use?

Meanwhile, DoorDash paid a lot less for Instacart today – $17 billion – than the rumored $40 billion it was considering back in 2021.

But Instacart isn’t a failed business. It’s growing. On the day the deal was announced, Instacart reported third-quarter orders up 10% and advertising and other revenue up 23% year over year. A company can be doing well and still benefit from a broader path to its next customer.

A broader ecosystem helps in a few ways. DoorDash would give Instacart’s grocery capabilities more occasions for use, from the dinner order to the convenience run. And the companies say Instacart customers would gain access to more than 50,000 additional stores. It could also put Instacart’s tools within reach of more retailers, including stores that couldn’t justify building them on their own.

The companies expect the deal to close in late 2027 or the first half of 2028, which leaves time to show retailers what they’ll get.

DoorDash has committed that local grocers can work with any delivery platform they choose after the deal closes. I’d also want to see measurement that holds up across both apps and a retailer’s own site, as well as ad tools designed for the smallest merchants rather than scaled down from enterprise tools built for the largest.

Whether the deal proves successful depends on whether retailers gain profitable incremental sales while spending less time managing the tools.
