“Changing Tottenham history”: Roberto De Zerbi ignites after historic success at Chelsea

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Roberto De Zerbi finally has his benchmark match at Tottenham. Traveling to Stamford Bridge this Saturday, Spurs struck a major blow by winning against Chelsea (0-2), after a particularly successful performance. After a very complicated start to the season, marked by disappointing results and growing pressure around its coach, the London club showed a much more attractive face. Dominant in the game, the visitors notably finished the match with 70% possession, their highest total in the Premier League since April 2025. Long held back by a lack of efficiency, they finally found the fault at the end of the match thanks to an error by goalkeeper Mike Penders, before Mathys Tel came to seal this precious success. A victory all the more symbolic as it puts an end to a long period of drought on the Blues’ pitch, where Tottenham had not won in the league since April 2018.

Beyond the three points, this success could mark a real turning point for De Zerbi’s men. Spurs not only ended a series of six consecutive defeats against their London rival in the Premier League, but they also kept their clean sheet at Stamford Bridge for the first time since December 1986. A strong signal for a team which was still finding its bearings since the start of the year. The collective mastery, the intensity displayed and the ability to remain solid despite Chelsea’s attempts to react have enough to restore confidence to a group lacking certainty. Obviously relieved after this performance, Roberto De Zerbi did not hide his enthusiasm at the final whistle. The Italian now sees further and displays great ambitions for the future, with the desire to restore Tottenham to a place worthy of its history, even if it means sending a strong message to the entire championship.

Relieved by this prestigious success, Roberto De Zerbi especially wanted to highlight the collective progress of his team. The Italian coach believes that Tottenham are turning a corner, both in the game and in the state of mind. “ It was a great moment for the supporters, the club and the players. They deserved this victory and had prepared very well. We are making a lot of progress in our mentality, as a group and in our relationships with the players. We are also making progress on the ground. Now we are a complete team. The players are playing better and we have improved our style of play. I have to believe in this group. I never lost confidence in my players. We must continue to work hard and improve. We have a lot of great players, and I believe we can accomplish something important if we continue on this path. The atmosphere in the locker room is perhaps more important than tactics “, declared the Italian coach at a press conference.

This transformation, however, is not limited to tactical adjustments. De Zerbi particularly insisted on the importance of the work carried out behind the scenes, with a week spent in Marbella to strengthen ties and restore trust. “ When we eat dinner together and spend time together in the locker room, I see a different group, different relationships and a different vibe among the players. The team showed great character today, but we have a lot of new players. Those who have stayed since last season and two or three seasons ago have suffered results, notably two 17th places. We are working to change the last page of the club’s history. We are fortunate to have many great people in the locker room and many great players. I believe that by working hard we can achieve something important. The week in Marbella, face-to-face exchanges, opening up and removing the mask, were important for morale and faith », concluded the former OM coach. Tottenham may have finally found the trigger. It now remains to confirm this rebirth over time.

8 Books to Read if You Love Prime Video's ‘Reacher'

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Ever since it premiered its first season back in 2022, Nick Santora’s Reacher has been a major streaming hit and pop culture phenomenon, becoming one of Prime Video’s flagship shows. Based on Lee Child’s Jack Reacher novels and starring Alan Ritchson in the lead role, the series has been a consistent success with both critics and audiences for years now, and the show has developed a massive fan following. And if you’re a Reacher fan hungry for more like it, it’s time to hit the bookstore.

The obvious choice for more Reacher is, of course, the Jack Reacher book series, the 31st of which was released in October 2026, written by Child’s brother Andrew Child. But beyond those, there are a lot of great books out there that feature characters similar to Reacher and/or explore comparable stories of crime, action, and mystery. Without further ado, here’s our handpicked selection of books to read if you love Reacher.

Arguably the most Reacher book that’s not a Reacher book, David Baldacci’s Zero Day is the first novel in the author’s John Puller series. Like Reacher, Puller is the perfect combination of brains and brawn — built like a mountain and the smartest guy in most rooms — and he applies those talents in service of his country as the best investigator in the US Army’s Criminal Investigations Division. The novel follows him on a shocking case in remote West Virginia that he soon suspects may be part of something much bigger than he initially anticipated.

Baldacci has built quite a reputation as an author of highly suspenseful thrillers, and Zero Day is fairly representative of his work. It’s a genre book, the kind you might pick up in an airport bookstore, and a masterfully executed one at that. With a charismatic protagonist and plenty of twists and turns to keep you on your toes, Zero Day and its three sequels are an excellent read for fans of Reacher.

Created by Robert Crais, Elvis Cole and Joe Pike are Los Angeles-based private investigators introduced in the 1987 novel The Monkey’s Raincoat. Since then, the series has expanded to include nineteen more books, all following Cole and Pike into L.A.’s sordid underbelly in various cases, some of which tie back to their own pasts. The latest book in the series, The Big Empty, was released in 2025.

Robert Crais may not be as internationally popular as Lee Child, but the author has been hailed by many as one of America’s best crime writers. His Elvis Cole and Joe Pike novels are easily among the most engaging detective books of all time, with decades' worth of stories to explore. Witty, surprisingly emotional, and anchored by a charismatic duo who are perfectly matched, the Elvis Cole and Joe Pike series is a delightful read for all fans of sharp crime fiction.

Vince Flynn’s Mitch Rapp is a modern literary action hero who has appeared in 20+ novels since his first appearance in 2010’s American Assassin. Capture or Kill isn’t one of Flynn’s books, but it is the first Mitch Rapp novel written by the franchise’s current author, Don Bentley. Set in 2011, the book revolves around the United States government’s efforts to capture or kill Osama bin Laden. A highly effective CIA assassin, Mitch Rapp is brought in to gather crucial intel and foil the plans of an Iranian foe bent on upending the power structures of the Middle East.

Though perhaps not as popular as the Reacher franchise, the Mitch Rapp series is a successful line of thriller novels, and Capture or Kill marks the beginning of an exciting new era for the series. Blending real-life facts with gripping spy fiction, the novel is an excellent read that stays true to the late Vince Flynn’s style while expanding the world in interesting ways. Since its publication in 2024, Capture or Kill has been followed by two sequels, 2025’s Denied Access and 2026’s Double Tap, both written by Bentley.

Four action series. One of them is built around exactly the way you handle pressure, people, and a room that has just gone very quiet. Here is where you would survive the trilogy, and probably headline it.

You are precision with a grudge. You don't posture, you don't escalate, and you don't warn anybody twice. That is exactly why the people who know you are so careful never to be the reason you start.

You understand the thing the whole franchise is secretly about: the cars are spectacular, but the crew is the point. You collect people, you keep them, and you'd drive through a building for any one of them.

You over-prepare and then take the dangerous part yourself anyway. It looks like control, but it's really responsibility. You have never worked out how to ask somebody else to carry the risk.

You are not the most trained person in the building, the best equipped, or the one anybody would have picked. You are simply the one still standing at the end, which turns out to be the only qualification that matters.

Written by Spanish author Juan Gómez-Jurado and translated into English by Nicholas Caistor, Red Queen is a mystery thriller novel that’s the first book in the Antonia Scott series. The story introduces Antonia Scott, the daughter of a British diplomat and a Spanish mother, who has a uniquely gifted mind that allows her to see connections others do not. After a personal tragedy, Antonia attempts to retreat from the world, but she’s brought out of her self-imposed retirement by a terrifying new case.

Often compared to Stieg Larsson’s Millennium trilogy (and its protagonist, Lisbeth Salander), Gómez-Jurado’s Antonia Scott novels are easily among the best thriller books of the past decade. An international bestseller that’s also earned high critical acclaim, Red Queen is a fast-paced crime read with the right balance of chills, thrills, humor, and emotion. The book was followed by two sequels, Black Wolf and White King, and a thriller series based on the novels, also titled Red Queen, premiered on Prime Video in 2024.

A classic political thriller novel written by Frederick Forsyth, The Day of the Jackal revolves around a professional assassin who is hired by French dissidents to kill Charles de Gaulle, the president of France. As the Jackal makes preparations for the kill, investigators uncover details of the plot and race against time to capture the assassin before he can finish the job. The book is inspired partly by true events — there was indeed an attempt to assassinate de Gaulle in 1962, which the book opens with — but the actual story is a work of fiction.

The Day of the Jackal was a big hit when it was first published in 1971, winning the Edgar Award for Best Novel in 1972. The novel has remained consistently popular ever since and has inspired multiple film and TV adaptations around the world, most recently the 2024 Sky-Peacock series starring Eddie Redmayne and Lashana Lynch. The Day of the Jackal is a thriller classic that’s highly suspenseful and thoroughly engrossing, and easily one of the most iconic novels in its genre.

Created by Robert Ludlum, the Bourne series is a collection of spy novels that started with 1980’s The Bourne Identity. The books follow Jason Bourne, whose story begins with him being found by fishermen in the Mediterranean Sea, with no idea who he is or how he got there. As he slowly recalls the pieces of his lost past, Bourne feels compelled to hunt down an international terrorist and finds unsettling evidence that he may be an assassin himself.

The Bourne novels and the movies they inspired are very different beasts entirely. Where the films focus on high-intensity action, the novels are gripping Cold War thrillers that are more about tradecraft and intrigue than just violence and explosions. Ludlum wrote three of the Bourne books, including 1986’s The Bourne Supremacy and 1990’s The Bourne Ultimatum. After his death, the series has been continued by authors Eric Van Lustbader and Brian Freeman, with the latest (and 22nd) novel, The Bourne Revenge, released in 2026.

Written by Gregg Hurwitz, Orphan X is a thriller novel that’s the first book in the series. The novel follows Evan Smoak, a former government assassin who helps people in need, going by the codename “The Nowhere Man." When a meeting with a client leads Evan into an ambush, he finds himself hunted by the program that created him.

A pulpy modern thriller hit that captures the gritty energy of genre classics like the Bourne series and the Jack Ryan novels, Orphan X is a thoroughly absorbing read from start to finish. Thrilling and suspenseful, with plenty of action, the book is an entertaining introduction to one of the best novel series of the 21st century. Today, the Orphan X series includes 12 books. There have been multiple attempts to adapt the novels for film or television, though none of them have panned out just yet.

Written by Tom Clancy, Patriot Games is the second published book in Clancy’s Jack Ryan series and the first chronologically. The novel follows Ryan, a former U.S. Marine lieutenant, who foils a kidnapping attempt on the Prince and Princess of Wales while holidaying in London. The incident makes him a target of the Irish terrorist group behind the failed kidnapping, putting his family in danger.

Jack Ryan and Reacher have been popularly compared and contrasted for practically as long as both have been around; especially these days, with Prime Video boasting hit adaptations of both. But though Reacher and Jack Ryan may share a first name and military connections, the two book series are vastly different, with the Jack Ryan books (Patriot Games, in particular) taking a more cerebral approach that reflects the geopolitics of their time. It may not be the actual first book of the series, but Patriot Games is an excellent introduction to Jack Ryan for new readers. The novel also inspired a film adaptation starring Harrison Ford in 1992.

Binance Hacker Continues Aggressive Bitcoin Buying Spree

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The hacker behind the monumental Binance attack is still buying up more Bitcoin (BTC).

As per the latest blockchain analytics data from Lookonchain dated Nov. 4, the hacker continues to exchange SOL for Bitcoin, moving the stolen assets across several crypto wallets.

The blockchain tracker added that the hacker is using the following multi-step process to exchange the stolen crypto for Bitcoin.

The hacker’s complicated scheme involves splitting his SOL assets among different wallets before selling the SOL for USDC, transferring the USDC from Solana to Ethereum, buying ETH using the USDC and finally exchanging the newly bought ETH for BTC using THORChain, a decentralized cross-chain liquidity protocol.

This long and complicated cross-chain transaction process allows the hacker to easily transfer his funds between different blockchain networks. It also makes it harder to trace the stolen assets.

This in not the first time that the hacker has been buying Bitcoin with the stolen funds. In fact, he had earlier been seen making transactions to buy Bitcoin using tens of millions of dollars’ worth of stolen SOL.

As per Lookonchain’s data dated Nov. 1, the hacker behind the Binance hack was trying to convert approximately 970,522 SOL, worth roughly $95.9 million at the time of writing, into Bitcoin.

The hacker was even seen making a transaction involving 486,970 SOL (approximately $46.4M) in just one hour.

However, during these transactions, the hacker used a different service called Relay Protocol, which also allows cross-chain crypto transfers.

From the recent blockchain data, it looks like the hacker is still focused on Bitcoin. He’s also using several decentralized services to exchange the stolen crypto.

The latest official update from Binance shows that the massive security breach has affected crypto assets worth around $6.34 billion.

As per the update, the exchange says that it has frozen/intercepted about $2.85 billion worth of stolen cryptocurrency as of Nov. 3.

According to US federal authorities, the attack was conducted by a North Korean state-sponsored hacker group called TraderTraitor.

The FBI is also asking various decentralized crypto services like THORChain to prevent funds linked to the attack from moving around.

Even with all these efforts, the latest on-chain data shows that the hacker is still moving stolen funds across different networks.

Democrats Regain House and Senate in Midterms Defined by Affordability Concerns

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Democrats rode affordability concerns to major wins Tuesday, retaking control of the House and Senate and flipping multiple districts.

Democrats flipped Senate seats in Texas, Ohio, Kansas, Iowa, North and South Carolina and picked up a swath of seats in the House to win both chambers of Congress, according to AP projections. They've spent the year hammering Republicans on affordability and housing costs.

The 2026 elections, in which all of the 435 seats in the House and 35 in the Senate were up for election, as well as a swath of real estate and housing related measures. Both Democrats and Republicans have listed priorities to improve housing affordability in coming years.

"Tonight’s Democratic sweep shuffles control of the legislative branch, but the economic impact, which is fundamental for the U.S. housing market, is far less clear," says Realtor.com® Senior Economist Jake Krimmel. "Democrats have a clear mandate to focus on pocketbook issues, but whether they can translate that into a legislative agenda that delivers on that mandate remains to be seen."

Housing issues have been a dominant note in 2026, as Americans struggle with affordability. The 30-year fixed-rate mortgage hit its highest level in three years last month before dipping back to 7.24% last week, according to Fannie Mae. The national average gas price is $4.14 compared to $3.03 a year ago, according to AAA. President Donald Trump has pledged to focus on affordability issues.

"President Trump has been laser-focused on making housing more affordable," White House spokesman Davis Ingle told Realtor.com in a statement on Tuesday morning.

Ingle noted that Trump in his second term had signed an executive order prohibiting large Wall Street firms from purchasing single-family homes, directed Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac to purchase $200 billion in mortgage bonds that helped drive rates to four-year lows, and "cut unnecessary red tape at a historic pace to boost supply, speed construction, and lower costs."

"The President will not stop fighting until the American Dream of homeownership is within reach for every American, which is why he continues to sign bold new executive orders and calls on Congress to pass further legislation," he said.

Congress passed a bipartisan housing package aimed at making it easier to build homes, the 21st Century Road to Housing Act, this summer. But it will take years for some of those changes to take effect in the market. And lawmakers expect states and local governments must implement some changes.

The Trump administration, meantime, has undertaken other changes such as encouraging more innovation in the homebuilding sector and reducing rules for some housing, like manufactured homes. It's also sought to limit the impact of institutional investors in the housing market.

"I wasn’t on the Ballot and if I was, we would have won IN A LANDSLIDE!," Trump posted to X late Tuesday, as major news outlets projected Democrats would take control of the House after flipping half a dozen seats. Trump later told the New York Times he would accept the results of the election.

The economist Krimmel says that one "wildcard" to keep an eye on is how the election outcome impacts Trump's war with Iran.

"Many Democrats have been vocal critics of the conflict, which has sparked inflationary oil supply shortages," he says. "A resolution would likely improve oil supply, helping to ease price pressures and potentially providing relief for mortgage rates. However, a lot of uncertainty remains. Wanting the conflict to end, and finding terms that work for all involved are two different things."

Democrats marked the evening with several key wins in both the House and the Senate.

In South Carolina, Democrat Annie Andrews flipped the Senate seat formerly held by Republican Lindsey Graham. Andrews beat Graham's sister, Darlene Graham, who had been picked by Trump.

And Democrat James Talarico flipped a Texas Senate seat blue for the first time since 1988. In Kansas, Adam Hamilton became the first Democrat in almost a century to be elected to the Senate.

Democrats had several early victories. Democratic former North Carolina Governor Roy Cooper flipped the state's Senate seat, replacing Republican Thom Tillis, who chairs the Senate's Financial Institutions and Consumer Protection Subcommittee. Democrat Jamie Ager also flipped a North Carolina House district.

"We knew that the chaos and corruption in Washington were hurting our people," Cooper said in a speech. "Today, you said 'this has to stop.'"

Pennsylvania saw three formerly Republican seats picked up by Democrats including Janelle Stelson, Paige Cognetti and Bob Brooks. They ousted incumbents Ryan Mackenzie, Rob Bresnahan Jr. and Scott Perry.

In New Jersey, Tom Kean Jr. lost re-election after an extended absence fueled national headlines. Meantime, in Ohio Democrat Sherrod Brown returned to Congress after campaigning heavily against incumbent Jon Husted, attacking him over his prior support of data centers.

"Politics should never ever be about two private clubs who are more interested in beating up each other than they are in solving problems," said Josh Turek, who flipped a Senate seat in Iowa for Democrats.

On Truth Social, Trump posted a photo of a red hat with the word "veto." He has repeatedly criticized Democrats this year saying their policies drive up housing and other costs.

Trump told Fox News late on Tuesday night that he would not shy away from using presidential vetos to block "crazy" measures advanced by Democrats in Congress.

"I have the power of veto, and the power of veto, you know, it's impervious," the president said.

House Speaker Mike Johnson, who won election in Louisiana, defended Republicans' economic actions in remarks before midnight. He said Americans would "suffer" if Democrats use control of Congress to enact "retribution" over the next two years.

"From the first day of this election we knew that to keep the House and sustain the majority, we would have to defy more than a century of midterm history," Johnson said. "It's very, very difficult to overcome."

But Johnson said an agenda of "common sense" would lead Republicans to take back the House in 2028.

"Republicans have much more work to do," he said. "The new Congress doesn't change that at all."

Tristan Navera is a senior reporter on housing policy, covering trends and solutions in the housing market from Washington, DC. He was previously a senior reporter at Bloomberg Law, and before that covered real estate for the Washington Business Journal. Earlier in his career, he spent a decade reporting on business and real estate in Dayton and Columbus, OH. A Cincinnati native, he holds a journalism degree from Ohio University.

A bandicoot skull given to a museum by a widow in 1920 belongs to a species that went extinct before it had a name

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

A bandicoot skull donated in 1920 belongs to the little brown bandicoot, a new extinct species, in a study of 800 specimens that lists 7 Isoodon species.

Mrs. Symonds Clark lost her husband on July 10, 1920. In October she handed one of his bandicoot skulls to the South Australian Museum in Adelaide. It went into the drawers as “SAMA M1981, skull only, sex unknown,” and was later labeled as a golden bandicoot.

The ear capsules on it were far too small for that.

Kenny Travouillon of the Western Australian Museum and eight co-authors have now made SAMA M1981 the type of a new species, the little brown bandicoot, Isoodon microtis.

It most likely came from Eyre Peninsula and was gone before anyone noticed. Three skulls and some jaw fragments are all that is left of it.

The paper lists the Adelaide skull as probably female, going by its smaller canines. Two more skulls turned up at Museums Victoria in Melbourne under the label Isoodon sp., with no data except that they came from a Mr. Waterhouse.

DNA was tried on all three and none came back usable. So the species stands on skull and tooth characters. In the team’s combined analysis it lands next to the southern brown bandicoot, even though it shared Eyre Peninsula with a quenda.

Microtis is Greek for “small ear.” It is the smallest Isoodon in overall size. Its bullae are also smaller relative to the skull than in any other member of the genus.

Symonds Clark kept birds and studied them, and he sometimes collected mammal skulls for the museum. The team dated his skull to between 1876, his last logged donation, and 1914. That is the last entry in his diary, now at the State Library of South Australia.

None of the bandicoots in the diary could be matched to it for sure. His only trip out of Adelaide in those years was to Eyre Peninsula.

The Melbourne pair goes back to Frederick George Waterhouse, the museum’s first curator from 1861 until he resigned in February 1882.

His reports mention two bandicoots shipped to Melbourne between November 1866 and January 1867, which puts their collection between 1862 and 1866.

In those years the catalogue shows five bandicoot donations, from a stuffed one given by a Mr. L. Clark in July 1864 to two skins from a Mr. Andrews, and the localities were not always recorded.

The place came instead from subfossil jaws in Adelaide, three lower and two upper, from at least three animals. They were dug at the South Block Site near Edillilie, about 5 miles (8 kilometers) east of Coulta, in an area not cleared for agriculture.

Travouillon has said the work started in 2016, with visits to bandicoot collections in Europe and the United States.

The paper credits the Winston Churchill Memorial Trust and the Australian Biological Resources Study (ABRS) for funding his access to specimens. Steven Cooper and Terry Bertozzi of the South Australian Museum and Adelaide University came in later.

In the end the team examined more than 800 specimens in 11 Australian collections and in London, Paris, Genoa, Munich, Washington and New York. Every available type specimen of Isoodon was examined and sampled for DNA, according to the paper.

DNA came from 83 fresh tissue samples and 75 historical ones. Pieces of dried skin were soaked overnight before extraction. That work ran in a trace DNA lab at the Australian National University, set apart from the main lab to keep modern DNA out.

The Oz Mammals Genomics Initiative did the sequencing. After filtering, 2,050 gene regions were left, about 1.14 million DNA letters, and the specimen list is posted on Dryad.

Eleven Isoodon species have been described over time. The 1990s revisions brought that down to three, and studies in 2012 and 2018 raised it to five. Now it is seven.

The other new one is the New Guinea brown bandicoot, Isoodon moresbyensis, named from Port Moresby in 1877 and later lumped in with the northern brown bandicoot. Its DNA is deeply split from that species and its skull is much smaller.

The Kimberley golden bandicoot is now I. auratus bundilarri, from the Uunguu word for the animal on Wunambal Gaambera Country, and its type is a young male from the Mitchell Falls campground.

A south coast quenda, I. fusciventer halae, honors Marie Hale, the first person to pick it out. The rest come from Daw Island, St Francis Island, Marchinbar Island, Cape York and the Adelaide Hills.

Measurements and genes recently split Sydney’s funnel-web spider into three species. Old specimens also made up much of the 2025 haul of new species from museum cabinets.

The quenda, Isoodon fusciventer, used to be a Western Australian species. Bandicoots from the Mount Lofty Ranges, Kangaroo Island and the Nuyts Archipelago now belong to it as well. The western and South Australian groups separated around 800,000 years ago, the team estimated.

The mainland South Australian quenda is I. f. terrenus, Latin for “earthly,” since it is not an island form. Its type, SAMA M25880, is an adult male taken 6.7 miles (10.8 kilometers) west-southwest of Mount Barker.

The subspecies once ran from the Murray River to coastal Eyre Peninsula but is now down to the Mount Lofty Ranges, Fleurieu Peninsula and Kangaroo Island.

That shrinks the southern brown bandicoot, Isoodon obesulus obesulus, to the coast from Sydney to southeast South Australia. It is already listed as endangered federally, and the authors call for an urgent review of its status.

Australia has lost 42 mammal species since European colonization, and nearly 20 percent were bandicoots and bilbies, the paper notes.

Foxes, cats, land clearing and changes to traditional fire practices are blamed. DNA testing has also shown that feral cats kill more native animals than expected.

There is no date for the extinction. The last of the skulls was in the museum by October 1920. Land clearing for farms was well under way on Eyre Peninsula in the late 1800s.

If the species lasted until foxes arrived, the authors wrote, “it is very likely that foxes would have played a role in its extinction.”

Marchinbar Island’s golden bandicoot is known from just two specimens, and no genetic samples exist for the Cape York northern brown bandicoots. The IUCN Red List was last updated for Australian mammals in 2016.

The authors suggest that I. f. terrenus, which shared Eyre Peninsula with the little brown bandicoot, could be reintroduced there, though “it would be difficult to find an ecological analogue to replace it.”

Craig Moritz and an anonymous referee reviewed the paper, which went in on December 17, 2025, and came out on October 14, 2026. SAMA M1981 is still in Adelaide, now as the type of its own species.

The full study was published in the journal Australian Journal of Taxonomy.

Tolkien Had a Deeper Meaning Behind These 3 Classic Lord of the Rings Characters

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

There is no denying that, for J.R.R. Tolkien, The Lord of the Rings was always going to deeply reflect his religious convictions. He expressed as much in a 1953 letter to a fan, collected years later in The Letters of J.R.R. Tolkien, noting, “The Lord of the Rings is, of course, a fundamentally religious and Catholic work; unconsciously so at first, but consciously in the revision.”

Thus, it is not surprising that many of the themes, plot points, and characters expressed in his legendarium echo his Christian beliefs. But while the story of Middle-earth is expressly not allegory, some have pointed out that we see these ideas embodied best in the form of the epic's three most essential characters — Gandalf, Frodo, and Aragorn — who uniquely speak to different aspects of Jesus Christ.

Unlike how his longtime friend C. S. Lewis intended Aslan in The Chronicles of Narnia to literally be Christ manifested in another world, Tolkien actively avoided such direct religious ties. Instead of inserting direct parallels between his mythology and the Bible, Tolkien crafted Gandalf, Frodo, and Aragorn to embody different parts of Christ's role as the Messiah. Philosopher Peter J. Kreeft, author of The Philosophy of Tolkien: The Worldview Behind The Lord of the Rings, points out this very idea when writing for Ignatius Insight. Kreeft notes that all three characters experience a "death and resurrection" arc and are portrayed as saviors who sacrificially "save all of Middle-earth from the demonic sway of Sauron." As he concludes, "They exemplify the Old Testament threefold Messianic symbolism of prophet (Gandalf), priest (Frodo), and king (Aragorn)."

While the New Testament teaches that Jesus Christ fills each of these roles, Tolkien splits the offices individually. Beginning with the prophet, Gandalf plays the part quite well. The wandering wizard (who is technically akin to an angel) is famously a "servant of the Secret Fire." He calls Frodo out of his comfortable life in the Shire, convinces him to have mercy on Gollum, and performs miraculous feats reminiscent of the biblical prophets of old. Indeed, his wisdom and counsel often border on prophecy, offering comfort or conviction when needed. Gandalf also most perfectly embodies the Christian concept of resurrection. Though he dies to save his allies, he is reborn in white robes, evoking the image of Christ's return in the Book of Revelation.

On the other hand, Frodo represents Jesus' priestly nature. He takes away the evil from Middle-earth by delivering the One Ring to its doom, willing to suffer the burden as he intercedes for all creation. Along the way, he is betrayed, beaten, and even stabbed in the side (as Christ was on the cross), and yet he proceeds with his providential task. In the end, Frodo leaves the world for heavenly places upon the completion of his earthly work.

Finally, Aragorn clearly embodies Christ's messianic role as king. Just as Jesus comes from the line of King David, making him the rightful heir to the throne of Israel, Aragorn rightly takes his place as ruler of Gondor in the aptly named The Return of the King, centuries after the prophecies had foretold it. Of course, he comes only after he first conceals his true identity, unveiling his lineage when the time is right. His willingness to pick up this task, partnered with his ability to command the respect and loyalty of those around him, further embraces this idea. It's no coincidence that Aragorn leads an army of the dead against Sauron's vile forces (another nod to Revelation), and even heals the wounded by the laying on of hands.

Although The Lord of the Rings is not meant to be read as religious allegory, there's no denying that Tolkien's Christian faith was of chief inspiration (and importance) when crafting it. There's something to be said as well that there had to be three Christ figures in this story, not just to echo the three messianic roles, but also because of the Christian belief in a triune God: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. Indeed, Tolkien's writings did not entirely ignore the existence of a God in Middle-earth either. When unraveling the history of Arda (the name for "Earth" in Tolkien's work) in The Silmarillion, the author reveals that Eru Ilúvatar is the "One" behind the Sacred Fire that created all things. Many during the Second Age, dubbed "The Faithful," worshiped Ilúvatar.

Even behind the scenes, Tolkien could not entirely divorce the events of The Lord of the Rings from the hand of providence. In a 1956 letter likewise collected in The Letters of J.R.R. Tolkien, the author reveals that the climactic destruction of the One Ring was, itself, something of a miracle. After Samwise Gamgee defeats Gollum's repentance (an act that Tolkien once described as the book's most tragic), the creature returns after Frodo succumbs to the power of the ring.

Unlike Christ, Frodo is unable to resist the temptation, and thus, Tolkien writes, "The Other Power then took over: the Writer of the Story (by which I do not mean myself), 'that one ever-present Person who is never absent and never named' (as one critic has said)." The author implies that, while no one would have made it as far as Frodo did, it was Ilúvatar that caused Gollum to slip and the One Ring to meet its doom.

Time would fail us to make note of all the Christian illustrations Tolkien wove into The Lord of the Rings. Be it Galadriel's connection to the Catholic interpretation of the "Virgin Mary" or the Luciferian downfall of the villainous Morgoth, there are too many to make note of here. Yet, there is no denying that portions of Christ can be found in Middle-earth, largely in the form of a wandering wizard, an unlikely hobbit, and a warrior born to be king.

Google Plays Hardball With Schoolchildren; Reddit Upvotes Search Ads

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Schools looking to ban AI for students are realizing just how much weight Google has to throw around. It’s a story publishers and ad tech companies know well.

As more K-12 schools ban AI, Google is playing ball by turning off the AI features in its products (unless school admins actively opt in to using them). But, because Google has so thoroughly baked AI into its search experience, Google has determined that turning off AI for schools also means turning off search, The New York Times reports.

Call it malicious compliance on Google’s part. After all, Google is kind of known as the search platform (as in, monopolistically). But, if you don’t want its AI, you don’t get its search platform either.

Taking away access to Google search makes the new policy feel like a way “to force AI on us all,” Morgan Polikoff, an education professor at the University of Southern California, tells the Times.

Polikoff may be onto something. After all, Big Tech has untold billions invested in the idea that the next generation must use AI.

By giving schools an ultimatum between permitting AI use or losing access to the largest search engine in the world, says former FTC chair Lina Khan, “Google is making clear it will punish those who don’t fall in line.”

Less than a week after announcing its shift to login-only viewing, Reddit is now beginning to test ad placements in its own search pages, the company shared in a blog post on Tuesday.

Reddit’s search ad algorithm will prioritize contextual relevance. For example, “A search for ‘espresso station’ could surface ads for espresso machines, coffee beans, or mugs,” the blog post reads.

Reddit has long planned to build up its own search platform, since its relationship with Google Search is on the outs.

As Reddit admitted during its Q3 earnings call last week, its search traffic dropped precipitously in October because Google has been sending less users there (or anywhere, really, thanks to the growing prominence of AI Overviews). That’s despite Google relying on Reddit’s content for AI scraping and training purposes.

Meanwhile, Reddit’s ad revenue has grown exponentially, with the company topping a $3 billion run rate for the first time this year. And now that logins will soon be required, users might be more inclined to use Reddit’s internal search engine rather than going to Google and typing, say, “espresso station reddit.”

Maybe there’s a silver lining for Reddit’s notoriously anti-ad user base, though. If marketers can simply buy more kinds of ad inventory on Reddit search, maybe they won’t spend so much time trying to astroturf and spam the actual content of subreddits.

Versant Media Group – the portfolio of networks that Comcast spun off in January – announced its third-quarter earnings on Tuesday. And the results are a mixed bag.

Versant saw a 4% year-over-year drop in revenue to $1.59 billion last quarter, which the company chalks up to general declines in the pay TV industry.

On a better note, platform revenue – which represents revenue from nonlinear sources, such as direct-to-consumer and streaming offerings – was up 10% YOY last quarter.

And it wouldn’t be a TV network earnings report without mention of the live sports biz. Versant closed its acquisition of Full Swing last quarter, building on its existing golf programming portfolio from Comcast, including Golf Channel and GolfNow. As TV publishers compete to grow their live sports offerings, Versant wants to claim golf as its differentiator.

Apple adjusts its App Store policies to restrict dating apps and AI companions to users 18 and up. [TechCrunch]

Shopify app extensions have been using subtle checkout page redirects to fool people into forking over payment and personal info. [Adweek]

Why publishers feel like Meta is holding them “hostage” over a planned terms of service change that would opt any site posting on the platform into AI scraping. [Digiday]

Netflix is launching a new interface called AI Discover to provide more targeted content recommendations to users. [THR]

Disney plans to eliminate thousands of jobs, giving managers a goal of 20% reductions. It’s Disney’s fourth round of layoffs this year. [Bloomberg]

There is a powerful undercurrent of disaffection and outright anger from the American populace toward US sporting institutions right now. But this isn’t just about logos cluttering the playing field. Sports leagues and teams are making decisions that sacrifice player safety and the enjoyment of their own fans in various bids for marketing revenue.

People Inc.’s referral traffic from Google search is down 70% over the past two years, and by 26% in Q3 alone. But its digital revenue is still growing, albeit slowly.

On Tuesday, Chord, a six-year-old commerce tech startup, acquired Zenlytic, an AI startup with an anthropomorphized data analyst agent named “Zoë,” for $5 million.

If you ask the executive leadership of Affle and Perion why the two companies are merging, the answer you’ll get is “synergies.”

A combo of Tubi, sports and political ads powered Fox’s growth this last quarter, even as Fox News lost viewers and the Roku deal awaits DOJ clearance.

It’s fall, and M&A is in the air. Two acquisitions happened this week: Stagwell bought Freestar, and DoorDash bought Instacart.

Good ads have a new name, ‘entertainment-first’ and SharkNinja is using it for its holiday campaign

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Entertainment-first has spent the year working its way into most conversations about marketing, including a fair few of Digiday’s. SharkNinja’s holiday campaign is the latest example.

Last year’s spot had Kevin Hart moving in next door to David Beckham. This year he’s house-sitting for him and he invites Tom Brady and Aryna Sabalenka over. Things get broken an each star then posted a video denying it was them.

The evidence started leaking on social media over the weekend as grainy security camera footage. In the the full spot, out today, Sabalenka drinks straight from the spout of a Ninja slushie machine. Brady wonders about one of the company’s skincare face masks and treads on a present, while Hart cracks a picture frame with a Shark vacuum. Beckham watches the whole thing on his phone live from his own security cameras.

Asked what makes that entertainment-first, Laura Dyer, senior vp of global brand and partnerships at SharkNinja, said it comes down to whether people want to share the ad and talk about it. Anything else is “educational”. That’s a test most memorable advertising has always passed. The fact that marketers now feel obliged to give it a name says something about where the industry has ended up. After years of ads built to satisfy a performance dashboard, making something people would choose to watch has become a position a brand has to declare.

SharkNinja has run this play before. When Brady joined last October, a teaser had the internet expecting a second Netflix roast before the reveal: he was roasting a chicken in a Ninja air fryer. The holiday campaign runs the same trick with a bigger cast and a Christmas tree.

Leaked “CCTV” clips and denial videos from the stars started going up on their accounts and SharkNinja’s at the start of the month two days before the full spot aired today.

“If you make it too long people lose a little interest,” said Dyer on the decision to make the ad’s rollout short. The point is to get people guessing, she continued. “We want people to start speculating, asking ‘what is this from SharkNinja? Where are they? Why are they denying being in David Beckham’s house?’,” Dyer added. Once the spot drops it goes to TV while local brand ambassadors in Italy, France, Germany and other markets will run their own versions under the same “there’s no home like a SharkNinja home” banner, with extensions running through to the end of the year.

The trickier part is that this is still an ad for appliances however it’s dressed up. Somebody still has to tell shoppers what the vacuum does and why it’s worth the money, and the holiday shopping season is when that matters most for businesses like SharkNinja. Dyer and her team tucked those answers into the ads themselves. For instance, Hart vacuums Beckham’s house with the machine bent in the middle so it reaches under a table, a SharkNinja party trick.

“The product is always the hero,” said Dyer. Indeed, the campaign will be judged on sales as much as brand equity so those details matter. Anyone who clicks through from the ad lands on a homepage selling Hart, Beckham and Sabalenka’s favorites.

For a brand that has spent the year being held up as a creator marketing case study, putting Beckham, Hart and Brady at the top of its holiday push is an unexpected call. Dyer called it a matter of fit. “I wouldn’t say it’s conscious at the exclusion of content creators,” she said. The creators haven’t gone anywhere either. They’ll be seeded with the campaign through the season and their videos of kitchens crammed with SharkNinja kit gave the team the idea in the first place.

SharkNinja made the spot without an agency. Its in-house team Creative Studios wrote and produced it while also working directly with each celebrity’s team. The in-house team also made last year’s neighbourhood spot, though that one was done in close partnership with HartBeat — Hart’s own production company. This time SharkNinja did “pretty much all of it by ourselves” Dyer said. That isn’t necessarily a sign of things to come, to be clear. If an agency makes sense, SharkNinja will hire one, she continued, and if it doesn’t the company is happy to do it itself because “our team is talented,” Dyer added.

Beneath the celebs, social media rollout and creators is an ambition the ad business has chased since the jingle.

Dyer described the ideal reaction as people turning to reach others and saying, “this is so funny, you should watch it.” Nobody had to brand that strategy when there were three channels and the ad break was the only way back to the programme. Audiences now skip, scroll and pay extra to get rid of ads so a brand making something people might watch on purpose has to say so out loud. Calling it entertainment-first is mostly an admission of how much of the rest people have learned to ignore. The point rang throughout Advertising Week New York last month.

“Oftentimes we are incredibly comfortable in our industry to waste quadrillions of dollars on things that fake reports justify, though everyone in this room knows it is garbage, “said Gary Vaynerchuk, CEO and chairman at VaynerX. “We are petrified to spend some pennies on something that everyone in this room knows is happening and is real, yet we don’t have the corporate reporting internally yet to justify what’s actually happening.”

As creator marketing becomes a fixture in many brands’ marketing strategies, creators’ roles are shifting and expanding.

DoorDash and Instacart are joining forces. Now advertisers want to know what happens to costs, data and measurement.

Google search was the line item buyers never questioned. That’s changing.

Case for and against – sticking with a Publicis-owned LiveRamp

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

In the months since Publicis Groupe announced its $2.2 billion deal to acquire LiveRamp, CMOs have been considering a crucial question: should I stay, or should I go? That deal closed this week (Monday Nov. 2), bringing the decision to the top of many marketers’ to-do lists.

To recap, clients and their agencies use LiveRamp to onboard their first party data and match it against a large dataset of audience profiles for media targeting and measurement. LiveRamp’s alternative ID solution, RampID, is one of the most commonly used by the market.

But the company’s acquisition by Publicis has prompted questions about the neutrality of this critical digital media infrastructure — and has brought older arguments over the usage of identity solutions back to the fore.

“It’s brought a lot more focus in the industry, both on the client side and certainly in agency land, on the importance and value of these foundational components,” said Jonathan Margulies, managing partner at Winterberry Group.

Here’s the case for and against clients sticking or twisting with LiveRamp.

Upon the deal’s closure, Publicis released a video in which CEO Arthur Sadoun and chief strategy officer Carla Serrano spent six minutes laying out Publicis’ LiveRamp pitch — and attempting to defuse concerns over neutrality and independence of the adtech company’s services.

For Sadoun and Serrano, the pairing’s chief benefit to clients was its “plug and play” nature — the idea that, in using LiveRamp and Epsilon, either as a client of the broader Publicis constellation or not, clients can save upfront investment and act faster. “Clients are trapped between two bad options: surrender their data to the walled gardens for easy scale and lose control, or hire an army of consultants and lose time and a lot of money,” said Sadoun.

Clients that do decide to unplug LiveRamp from their marketing stack will find the search for a new partner an expensive and time-consuming process. LiveRamp is considered to be a market leader, with a huge dataset owing to its larger customer base and partnership deals with commerce networks.

Combining its capabilities with Epsilon makes its position even stronger. Given the IAB projects commerce media spending to grow 13.6% this year, making it the third-fastest growing area of advertising investment after social media and CTV — those capabilities, which are used on the supply-side by the likes of Walmart, Target, Albertsons and Kroger may become more important to CMOs in coming years. LiveRamp is also well-positioned for advertisers hoping to move early into emerging channels like ChatGPT; the firm became OpenAI’s first conversion API partner in June.

For many clients, those arguments will outweigh neutrality worries expressed by Publicis’ holdco rivals (which aren’t, in any case, united in their opposition. Havas, for example, seems set to continue using LiveRamp’s tech in its flagship Converged data platform in North America.)

Furthermore advertisers can now rely upon FTC-enforced rules of the road. Publicis is required to maintain access to LiveRamp by competitors, to set up firewalls to ensure it doesn’t access customer data or confidential business information, and to permit an independent auditor to probe its compliance every six months for two years, and then annually for three years (it doesn’t, however, prohibit the firm from developing integrated products that bridge LiveRamp and its other business units). “It will stay open to every brand, every platform, and yes, every agency,” said Serrano.

While it’s unusual to find companies that commonly spar over new business pitches clients of each other, not every holdco chief is queasy about the prospect. Yannick Bolloré, CEO of Havas, said earlier this year he expected his group to stick with LiveRamp.

“We’re clients of Publicis now,” he told Adweek in July. “We still work with them. Our strategy is to partner with the best data company to offer the right data sets for clients, whether it is LiveRamp, or Epsilon, or Acxiom, or Kantar, or whatever.” If Bolloré can put up with it, can’t you?

Publicis’ ambitions for LiveRamp are tied up in earlier investments in companies like Lotame, Epsilon and Sapient, through which it wants to position itself as a one-stop shop for the entire media investment process. For advertisers already working with its agencies the addition of LiveRamp into the mix is a plus. But for clients that don’t work with Publicis, the neutral status of firms like LiveRamp isn’t a minor issue.

Some CMOs are actively looking for an alternative destination. As Benoit Vatere, chief media officer at Liquid Death, put it in a Monday Linkedin post: “Who in their right mind will let a holdco own your data and graph. Do you really think we are that stupid?”

Agency discomfort over their rivals owning critical infrastructure is more than just a feeling. In 2018 IPG acquired Axciom’s marketing services business, leaving its identity resolution business — LiveRamp — as a separate entity, its independence preserved. Publicis’s 2026 deal has prompted its peers WPP and Omnicom to back off using the platform because they feel that independence has been compromised.

“I don’t see there is any way that you can get any value keeping LiveRamp independent of the rest of your infrastructure,” said John Wren, the CEO of Omnicom, back in May. Stagwell CEO Mark Penn has made similar noises. “They don’t want to indirectly provide a competitive advantage to one of their direct [rivals],” said Margulies.

For clients operating outside the United States, the downside is even larger. 95% of LiveRamp’s $800 million annual revenue comes from the U.S. market. The United States accounts for a huge proportion of digital ad spending, but brands also operating across international borders and regulatory domains might consider the neutrality problem too large a bugbear to bother with, given LiveRamp’s limited application elsewhere.

Their withdrawal could, in turn, impact advertisers that don’t work with the big three “operating companies”. If WPP and Omnicom’s client data is no longer present in LiveRamp’s dataset, then its reservoir shrinks – and its ability to link another advertiser’s first party information to the wider market is diminished.

Although Sadoun told analysts during Publicis’ recent earnings call he was unworried by the policy change, Apple’s recent crackdown on identity resolution has highlighted the limitations of relying on deterministic means of targeting audiences.

For those that want them, alternatives are in the offing. Last week WPP boss Cindy Rose was keen to highlight how its data collaboration platform InfoSum offered a means of patching proprietary data and consumer data secure from the meddling of Califoria tech giants — while Omnicom’s Axciom unit has been developing its own identity solution, Real ID, as a replacement for LiveRamp.

Media buyers have gradually begun relying less on third-party audience data in recent years. According to a OpenX study published earlier this year, 40% supply-side-targeted CTV deal spend processed through its platform in 2025 involved third party targeting data, down from 78% three years earlier. Contextual targeting and vector-based solutions have become more popular among brands like Heineken for targeting on the web or on CTV, while fewer advertisers rely outright on first-party data.

“This is driving brands and their partners to do a lot of soul searching of exactly what their strategy is going to be vis-a-vis data,” said Margulies.

In basic terms, more advertisers are embracing a probabilistic, intent-driven approach to addressable media. Such approaches have less need for the identity resolution solutions offered by LiveRamp. When added to neutrality or privacy concerns like Vatere’s, this shift makes it easy for advertisers to step away.

Google’s Q3 earnings reveal AI Mode’s impact on search advertising growth, plus ongoing diminishing returns from ad tech.

At DMBS, agency leaders explained why retail media offers new opportunities to harness closed-loop measurement in a way that benefits both upper- and lower-funnel marketing efforts.

Future of TV Briefing: What it costs brands to fund long-form creator series

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Future of TV Briefing covers the latest in streaming and TV for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Wednesday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

This week’s Future of TV Briefing looks at the economics behind brands sponsoring creator-led long-form original video series.

A creator-led long-form original video series can cost a brand anywhere from $150,000 to $5 million, according to Cyndi Steele, a former TV executive producer who leads talent management firm G&B Digital Management’s &Beyond Creators’ Studio.

A six- to seven-figure investment may be an eye-popping amount for CFOs wary of buying into creators. But paying that premium begins to make more sense as video platforms like YouTube, Instagram and TikTok have started highlighting serialized shows and as brands treat creators less like media properties and more like creative collaborators.

Fifty-seven percent of brands said that generating content is a goal for their creator campaigns, according to a study by LTK and Northwestern University released last month. Your reaction to that stat may be something like, “Isn’t generating content 100% the point of brands hiring creators for campaigns?” Yeah. But the shift underlying the stat is how brands are looking to squeeze more content out of the content that creators produce beyond social.

Recycling creator content to this extent requires a lot of content. Which is where the long-form series pitch can come in.

Consider a brand-sponsored, creator-led series that spans 10 long-form episodes plus three short-form videos per episode for a total of 40 pieces of content. That can average between $300,000 to $500,000, according to Steele. Again, that’s a pretty penny. But consider the cost for a brand to produce a traditional TV show.

“When I was in unscripted reality TV, let’s say 10 episodes of a 40-minute-long show, the budget for that is usually around like $5 million. That’s only to do literally the physical production, nothing else,” Steele said.

There are different levers a brand can pull on to adjust the costs of a creator-led long-form show. The episode count can be changed, for example. But the primary line items include:

IP ownership is the least settled aspect of the deal, as Digiday recently reported. For starters, it’s subject to how many ownership the creator retains as well as &Beyond’s ownership stake. The studio prefers first to agree to an ownership split with the creator.

“If it’s an original concept that we’re developing as the exclusive development and production partner, we do split ownership with our talent. A lot of times we like to have the talent typically have the majority ownership. So it a lot of times looks like a 60-40 or 70-30 [split],” Steele said.

But then where does the brand’s cut come from? Well, that becomes a re-appraisal of everyone’s equity. But only if the brand opts to pay for IP ownership.

“The way we’ve approached it so far is they’re not owning the IP. But we can set up specifically owned assets within there, and they can have first right of refusal for future seasons,” Steele said.

Then if a show succeeds and there are future seasons, the brand can pick back up the IP ownership conversation.

“From a brand’s perspective, I think a lot of them aren’t ready to fully dive in with a full IP investment at that level because it is something that takes a long time to really come back and to grow. But from our standpoint, it’s fine because we approach it [by] pulling a little bit from the model of how our managers are currently doing brand deals with the talent and then also how we experience doing brand integrations from the traditional side. It’s just a lot more flexibility,” said Steele.

“When a federal agency formally recognizes millions of creator-led businesses, the people who decide where money goes start to treat creators as an asset class.”

$1.3 billion: Roku’s platform revenue in Q3 2026.

2-3%: Percentage share of Netflix’s London-based employees who are being laid off.

$2.08 billion: Fox’s advertising revenue in Q3 2026.

$11.2 billion: YouTube’s ad revenue in Q3 2026.

20%: The cost reduction that Disney is aiming for as it plans more layoffs.

$373 million: Versant’s ad revenue in Q3 2026.

What DoorDash’s Instacart acquisition says about the modern delivery platform pitch

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This story was first published by Digiday sibling Modern Retail.

These days, delivery platforms have to offer more than just delivery services to brands and retailers.

DoorDash’s announced plans to acquire Instacart for $17 billion point to the constant need to gain market share by diversifying services and offerings.

Over the years, both DoorDash and Instacart have invested heavily in new services like AI assistants, advertising programs and in-store B-to-B solutions. DoorDash, in particular, has been on an expansion streak, acquiring U.K.-based competitor Deliveroo and restaurant reservations platform SevenRooms in 2025. Last year, DoorDash also acquired search ad startup Symbiosys for $175 million to deepen its advertising capabilities.

With the high cost of last-mile delivery, companies like DoorDash and Instacart have expanded well beyond delivering groceries and takeout.

Leah Sallen, U.S. head of CPG and retail strategy at programmatic ad tech platform MiQ, said the growing services and tools DoorDash has come to provide have made it more than just a marketplace for delivery. “Between delivery, grocery shopping, restaurant reservations and advertising, DoorDash is gaining access to more consumer touchpoints and insights into when, where and how people shop,” she said.

Over the past few years, DoorDash has aggressively built out its non-restaurant marketplace, partnering with major grocery, convenience and retail brands like Ulta, Skims and Macy’s. The company also introduced logistics features to solve pain points, like Dasher Returns. On the technology side, DoorDash has rolled out AI-powered tools, such as Ask DoorDash for shopping recommendations, DashBuddy for courier support and DashOS for restaurant customer management. These solutions are meant to create an all-in-one ecosystem for B-to-B infrastructure and logistics streamlining.

Now, the Instacart consolidation will bring DoorDash grocery retailers that the platform currently does not offer its customers, notably Walmart and Sam’s Club. But with that market share also comes challenges in maintaining retail relationships by offering more than just delivery solutions.

Neil Saunders, managing director of GlobalData, said DoorDash’s latest hefty acquisition will require further streamlining to make sense as a long-term investment.

Saunders said Instacart’s operations still largely rely on workers manually picking groceries. He said this is not only labor-intensive, but it’s also harder to scale efficiently as digital grocery orders continue to grow. “Either way, DoorDash should aim to position itself as a technology and infrastructure partner rather than simply a delivery provider,” Saunders said.

Some experts believe DoorDash’s proactive strategy is to simply keep up with Amazon’s delivery offerings. However, retail media networks have also become an important revenue source for delivery platforms in recent years.

At its surface, the DoorDash-Instacart deal is about two massive last-mile companies consolidating. But for advertisers, it could mean a broader disruption in retail loyalty, said Sallen, as the two platforms’ merger brings together deep data on restaurant, grocery and retail shopping.

“We have already seen this play out with TikTok Shop, which fundamentally changed consumer shopping behavior by using sophisticated algorithms to deliver highly personalized product recommendations,” Sallen said. A similar shift can happen with this acquisition, Sallen said, thanks to the combination of DoorDash’s immediate-consumption insights and Instacart’s long-standing grocery data.

Sallen explained that, similar to how Amazon became a starting point for shopping, consumers may increasingly turn to platforms like DoorDash to find the best product, price or delivery option before deciding which retailer to purchase from. In turn, she said, “That shift from selecting the retailer first to selecting the retailer last could prompt CPG brands to reconsider how they allocate their retail media dollars.”

Matt Gilbert, CEO of marketing platform Partnerize, agreed that the significance of a DoorDash and Instacart combination goes well beyond delivery.

“It’s about the growing concentration of consumer discovery, advertising, purchasing decisions and transaction data within a single commerce ecosystem,” Gilbert said. For consumers, that could mean greater convenience and fewer steps between discovering a product and buying it. “For advertisers, it creates an opportunity to connect marketing investment more directly to purchasing behavior across more categories and occasions.”

On the other hand, Gilbert said that when platforms increasingly control the consumer experience, the transaction and the reporting of advertising performance, advertisers need assurance that they’re measuring genuine incremental influence — “and not simply taking credit for purchases that would have happened anyway,” said Gilbert.

Sallen added that, as platforms like DoorDash add capabilities and AI increasingly influences shopping decisions, “The consumer path to purchase is becoming more diluted and harder for advertisers to follow.”

That shift could become even more pronounced as AI agents increasingly help consumers weigh price, proximity and product availability before deciding what and where to buy. Sallen said this could mean delivery behemoths like DoorDash could have an advantage in shaping AI-driven recommendations to millions of consumers. “The more intelligence it [DoorDash] can bring together, the more personalized it can make the shopping experience,” Sallen said.

“DoorDash’s growing portfolio of services could position it as a one-stop shop for retailers, with data becoming one of its biggest advantages,” Sallen added.

Publishers are pushing back against Meta’s AI scraping terms, testing their leverage over a platform they still rely on.

Political and news creators are relied on now more than ever, but what platforms are working for them? Which ones aren’t?

Digiday+ Research 2026 Creator Marketing Index: A brand guide to social media campaigns

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This research is based on unique data collected from our proprietary audience of publisher, agency, brand and tech insiders. It’s available to Digiday+ members. More from the series →

As creator marketing becomes a fixture in many brands’ marketing strategies, creators’ roles are shifting and expanding. Brands may pay creators to produce sponsored content, gift products to them in the hope of organic posts, or rely on a combination of creator- and brand-produced content published on their own social media accounts.

Overall, social media remains the leading channel for creator partnerships: 96% of respondents to Digiday’s first-quarter survey of brands and agencies said they partner with creators to create social media content. Within social media, Instagram (90%), Facebook (80%), YouTube (72%) and TikTok (63%) were the platforms marketers said they used most.

Across these social platforms, brands are taking various approaches to creator campaigns, from sponsored and gifted posts to content formats like product demonstrations, styled photos and event coverage. Understanding how these approaches perform can help brands make informed decisions about creator partnerships, content formats and campaign investment.

To measure the impact creator content has on brand campaigns, Digiday+ Research analyzed the engagement levels on leading creators’ social media campaigns from January to September 2026.

The Digiday Creator Marketing Index collected data from 1,755 social media posts and 20 branded social media campaigns, analyzing the top posts associated with each campaign’s hashtag and scoring them across a set of key dimensions to create a total index average score. Results are dependent upon the list of social media campaigns and the time period of data collection, generating a snapshot of the creator marketing landscape at a specific moment in time.

Digiday’s Index collected social media data from January to September 2026. Due to data restrictions, TikTok views were not captured. Data collection was conducted by Dash Social, an agentic social platform powered by brand-intelligent AI.

The social media campaigns were selected by Digiday’s editorial team.

When considering video content performance by platform, Instagram Reels received far more video views than YouTube’s Shorts and long-form videos, while TikTok videos had higher average engagement in later analysis. However, these results should be considered within the context of the Index’s focus on branded social media campaigns, meaning that the majority of posts included marketing-focused content rather than non-brand-related content, which performs the best on YouTube. Additionally, on YouTube — a video-first platform with a core strength in long-form content — most of the captured posts consisted of Shorts rather than long-form posts.

Average engagement rate — engagement divided by follower count — was only measurable on Instagram. When looking at average engagement rate by format type on Instagram, Reels performed the best among Instagram’s formats at an 11% engagement rate, underscoring the strength of short-form video on Instagram. Still posts came in second with a 7% engagement rate, followed by Carousel posts at 5%. Carousel posts can contain videos, but views were not counted as part of the average engagement rate for Carousel posts with video. Likewise, views were not counted as part of the average engagement rate for Still posts or Reels.

The top 75 posts associated with each campaign’s hashtag were identified based on overall engagement, including likes and comments across platforms. When Digiday analyzed those top-performing posts by platform, with engagement weighted equally across platforms, Instagram accounted for the largest share of posts associated with campaign hashtags.

However, TikTok posts within the top 75 generated higher average engagement than posts on other platforms. In other words, Instagram appeared more frequently among the highest-performing posts overall, while the top-performing TikTok posts generated greater engagement on average.

According to Digiday’s analysis, Instagram is a more consistent platform for performance, while TikTok has greater reach potential and a higher engagement ceiling. For brands running longer campaigns or prioritizing consistency, Instagram offers a more predictable environment for creator marketing. For smaller brands or those with limited budgets, TikTok is likely the better platform choice for creator marketing. While engagement on TikTok isn’t as consistent, its higher engagement ceiling gives creator content a better possibility of reaching a wider audience.

Looking more closely at sponsored and gifted posts, the Index defines them as follows. Sponsored posts refer to content created through a direct partnership between a brand and a creator. Gifted posts feature products that brands have sent to creators, typically as part of a PR effort, without a paid content agreement. Digiday classified posts as sponsored or gifted based on disclosures in captions, including paid labels, disclaimers, and hashtags such as #ad, #paid, #gifted and #PR. In some video posts, creators verbally expressed whether they had been paid by a brand.

The most common content types across both sponsored posts and gifted posts were product demonstrations and style shots. The Index defines a demo as content about a single brand or product that includes a description and shows a person using the item. A style shot is content that showcases a product in a scenic or visually styled setting without an explanation of how to use the item.

Style shots appeared more frequently in gifted posts than they did in sponsored posts, by a ratio of 19 to 15, indicating that creators favor that format when featuring products they have received for free without a formal content agreement. One possible explanation is that demos can appear to be overtly promotional since they feature one product as opposed to a “get ready with me” (GRWM) format that typically showcases multiple brands. Creators may want to avoid producing content that could be interpreted as advertising when it comes to gifted items, according to Digiday’s analysis.

When comparing average engagement rates, sponsored posts outperformed gifted posts across content types, with one notable exception: demos  — the most used content format type — where gifted posts performed best. Style shots displayed the opposite pattern, performing better in sponsored content where they also delivered the highest overall engagement among the content formats analyzed.

One potential factor behind this difference is the level of production and associated costs behind the content. Gifted style shots were often simple flat lays or product images, while sponsored style shots frequently included product videos. Brands often give creators larger budgets to develop content for sponsored product posts. That distinction is particularly important for product categories like fragrance, where brands rely on visual storytelling to communicate abstract qualities like mood and sensory experience.

While engagement provides a strong measure of how audiences respond to creator content, reach — calculated by average views — offers another important metric of performance. Digiday’s analysis found that sponsored posts had greater overall reach based on views than gifted posts, by a margin of 334,055 to 48,008. Within sponsored posts, style shots (707,646), demos (250,764) and influencer events (231,595) had the most views. Among gifted posts, demos generated the most views (81,023), well below sponsored formats.

That margin of difference is likely also linked to budget differences, according to Digiday’s analysis. Brands typically provide creators with paid media budgets to amplify sponsored posts beyond a creator’s existing audience, while gifted posts are limited to a creator’s own audience. As a result, sponsored content can generate both organic and paid reach, while gifted product content relies on organic distribution with more limited reach.

This Index exclusively analyzes brand-generated hashtags that incorporated either a brand name or a trademarked product name, such as #exaggereyes, #chocolatesoleil and #hydroboost.

The majority of the highest-ranked campaigns had a strong social media presence with a significant number of brand posts on the brand-owned handle, in addition to creator posts. By contrast, lower-ranked campaigns had a weaker presence on brand-owned channels, with no brand-generated posts in many cases. These findings suggest that while creator participation is an important driver of campaign success, brands’ own social media activity also plays a critical role, according to Digiday’s analysis.

Many of the highest-ranked campaigns also included a mix of gifted posts and sponsored posts. Gifted posts came from both macro- and micro-creators, indicating that high-ranked campaigns sent products to a wide range of content creators. However, investment was another important factor. All of the highest-ranked campaigns had large budgets backing them, highlighting the growing role of pay-to-play social media campaigns, where brands must invest in both creator partnerships and amplification to build reach.

Not all campaigns with paid support performed equally, however, suggesting that investment alone does not guarantee stronger performance.

Charlotte Tilbury’s #exaggereyes campaign ranked No. 1 in Digiday’s Index. One of its defining characteristics was the use of gifted posts: 35% of the campaign’s posts came from creators who received gifted products, the highest share among the campaigns Digiday analyzed.

Charlotte Tilbury’s collaboration with Influenster, a product sampling and review platform owned by Bazaarvoice, generated a large portion of the gifted posts for the #exaggereyes campaign. Rather than selecting creators and sending them PR packages directly, the brand relied on Influenster to collect user-generated content for its own use. Most of the gifted posts were demo videos, a format that Digiday’s analysis found generated higher average engagement within gifted posts than sponsored posts.

Neutrogena’s #hydroboost campaign ranked No. 2 in the Index and focused on a product-line extension. Neutrogena heavily invested in sending gifted products and directly collaborating with creators. As a result, the Hydro Boost campaign had the highest share of brand-seeded content — sponsored or gifted posts — at 39% of all posts.

The campaign also incorporated the broader #hyaluronicacid hashtag alongside the branded #hyrdoboost hashtag. This strategy allowed Neutrogena to not only position the Hydro Boost product as a line extension but also connect the campaign to the larger hyaluronic acid product category, potentially exposing the campaign to audiences with a broader interest in hyaluronic acid products.

Like Neutrogena’s campaign, Too Faced’s #chocolatesoleil campaign promoted a product-line extension but with a focus on the Brazilian market. Thirty-four of the top 75 posts associated with the Chocolate Soleil campaign hashtag mentioned Brazil, including content from Too Faced’s Brazilian social media account and other Brazilian creator accounts, indicating a deliberate focus on localizing the campaign for that market.

The campaign also had the lowest share of sponsored or gifted posts among the 10 highest-ranked campaigns, with 74 of its top 75 posts classified as organic posts. Instead, the campaign focused heavily on the product launch and activity generated through Too Faced’s brand handle to entice creators to test the product and create content about it.

Taken together, these campaigns show that there is no single formula for a successful brand-generated hashtag campaign. High-performing campaigns combined product seeding, creator partnerships, brand-owned content, organic activity and localized strategies in different ways. According to Digiday’s analysis, brand-generated hashtags can give brands greater ownership of the campaign conversation, particularly when tied to a specific product or launch, but their narrower focus may limit the volume and variety of content generated around them.

Making ads people want to watch is now a strategy. SharkNinja’s celebrity filled holiday spot shows why.

DoorDash and Instacart are joining forces. Now advertisers want to know what happens to costs, data and measurement.

Google search was the line item buyers never questioned. That’s changing.
