Former Fifa chair says ‘climate of fear’ could block challenges to Gianni Infantino’s re-election

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Gianni Infantino is set to run for re-election as Fifa president in March

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

A climate of fear could prevent national associations from contesting Gianni Infantino’s eligibility to serve a fourth term as Fifa president, the former chair of its governance committee has claimed.

Miguel Maduro, appointed by Infantino in 2016 amid pledges to reform Fifa following corruption scandals under predecessor Sepp Blatter, believes the three-term limit on presidents in Fifa’s statutes is clear and should be cited by associations to block Infantino from standing again next year.

Infantino’s position appeared under threat during the summer after details of a private investor plan for the World Cup, developed without the knowledge of the Fifa Council or national associations, emerged. However, he now seems well placed for re-election, drawing support from most associations across Africa, Asia, Oceania and South America.

Maduro, who was dismissed by Infantino in 2017 after challenging Russia’s Vitaly Mutko’s appointment to the Fifa Council, has helped campaign group FairSquare draft a letter to Fifa’s governance, audit and compliance committee questioning Infantino’s eligibility to stand again.

He is now backing its latest move urging national associations to declare him ineligible, but fears many may be reluctant to act.

“The fact that they are not (challenging Infantino’s eligibility) tells us a lot of the extent to which the climate is one of fear,” he said.

"We’ve seen that whoever acts on their own and challenges and shows a dissenting voice, or shows even that they have an opinion of their own, gets ostracised, gets set aside, loses influence in the framework of power of Fifa."

European associations may also hesitate to challenge Infantino on this issue, having agreed a change to Uefa’s statutes in 2024 which enabled incumbent Aleksander Ceferin to stand again next year.

On that point, Maduro noted: "There is certainly a contradiction in the spirit of what they’ve done, but it’s still different – at least (in Uefa’s case) they changed the rule.

"It was a very negative change but (the term limit preventing Ceferin standing again) is no longer in the rules. In this case (Fifa), it is in the rules."

Fifa said in 2022 that a decision taken by the Fifa Council just before the World Cup final had discounted Infantino’s first three-year term as president, leaving him clear to stand again in 2027.

The committee determining Infantino’s eligibility for next year’s election is chaired by Bruno Chiomento, who has known Infantino for more than 20 years. European members of the Fifa Council have asked Fifa to consider recusing him and replacing him due to his relationship with Infantino.

Should Fifa’s electoral committee clear him, Maduro said national associations could still challenge Infantino’s eligibility at the Court of Arbitration for Sport (CAS) and the Swiss Federal Tribunal beyond that.

Maduro believes Fifa’s checks and balances are even less effective now than under Blatter, arguing Infantino has been left "free to do whatever he wants".

He said reports of committee members like Chiomento receiving VIP trips to the World Cup were concerning if these were at the discretion of Infantino or Fifa rather than part of a contractual agreement.

"If those that are supposed to do such control are subject to being removed by those that they are supposed to control, their independence is undermined," he said.

James Talarico Flips Senate Seat In Texas, While Gina Hinojosa Takes Governor's Race

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The polls closed in Texas at 7 p.m. local time (the state is split between Central Time and Mountain Time) Tuesday, and within hours, one of the state's most contentious battles in the 2026 midterm elections was over, with Democrat James Talarico prevailing over Texas Attorney General Ken Paxton, giving Democrats their first statewide victory in Texas since 1994.

As part of a campaign in the state's most closely watched race, Paxton embarked on a whirlwind tour across Texas, joined by President Donald Trump along the way. Given Trump's slumping approval, combined with popular discomfort around Paxton's scandal-plagued career, Talarico's lead continued to grow even as Elon Musk pumped cash into the Republican side.

In June, political advisor Mark McKinnon described Talarico in a piece for Vanity Fair as “a rare talent.” “He’s an eighth-generation Texan, a former middle school teacher, and a Presbyterian seminarian. In a state where the Scriptures amount to a kind of civic fixture, Talarico out-Bibles many Republicans,” McKinnon wrote. "What’s more, he is preaching a new kind of message that is not along partisan lines. Here’s a sample: ‘The biggest divide in this country is not left vs. right. It’s top vs. bottom. Billionaires want us looking left and right at each other instead of looking up at them.’”

Democrat Gina Hinojosa defeated incumbent Greg Abbott in Texas's governor's race, becoming the state's first Latina governor, as well as the first Democrat to hold the office in more than 30 years. The last one was also a woman: Democrat Ann Richards, who served from 1991-1995. Richards was the mother of Cecile Richards, the longtime president of Planned Parenthood.

Forget 'Memento,' Billy Bob Thornton's Noir Thriller Is the Perfect Replacement

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The noir genre is present within the cinematic DNA of nearly every film by Joel and Ethan Coen, including their brilliant debut feature Blood Simple, the somber hardboiled mystery of Fargo, and even the The Big Lebowski's wacky sleuthing. Although it was evident that the Coens had deep admiration for the noir films of the 1940s, their 2001 film The Man Who Wasn’t There was a full-on throwback period piece, shot in black-and-white, that was heavily inspired by the thrillers of Alfred Hitchcock, most notably Shadow of a Doubt and Rope. It’s a film that contains the idiosyncrasies that are expected of the Coens, but also features one of their most transfixing and compelling protagonists ever in Ed Crane, a mild-mannered barber portrayed by Billy Bob Thornton. Thornton draws out the ambiguity within a character who is both completely vulnerable and entirely unreadable, and it's the different interpretations that can be spun out of The Man Who Wasn’t There that makes it one of the most underrated entries in the Coens’ entire body-of-work.

The Man Who Wasn’t There is set in 1949, where Ed works as a loyal employee at a business owned by his brother-in-law Frank (Michael Badalucco). Although Ed is a pragmatic investor, he is forced to come up with $10,000 to stake in dry cleaning technology that is proposed to him by his regular customer, Creighton Tolliver (Jon Polito). The Man Who Wasn’t There examines the ease with which an unassuming character can be drawn into conspiracy and murder when Ed, suspecting that his wife Doris (Frances McDormand) is having an affair with her boss, “Big Dave” (James Gandolfini), decides to blackmail him. It’s a murky, darkly comedic portrayal of the fractured state of the American psyche in the aftermath of World War II, and draws from both Hitchcock and other ‘40s classics to create a stirring thriller about the price of ambition.

A core tenant of many Hitchcock films is the notion of an “ordinary man in extraordinary circumstances,” and The Man Who Wasn’t There both adheres to and subverts these archetypes. It’s safe to assume that Ed served in the war and is dealing with some degree of post-traumatic stress disorder, but he is also clearly unprepared for a life of crime based on his clumsy self-defense against Dave. It’s similarly unclear if Ed’s drinking problem was the cause of his marital issues with Doris or if he developed his addiction as a result of it, and the loveless nature of their relationship makes his actions all the more fascinating. Ed is detached from reality in a way that is comparable to Meursault in Albert Camus's The Stranger, as he seems unaffected by violence and not particularly jealous as a result of Doris’ affair. Ed’s blackmailing of Dave is more of a strategic move than an act of vengeance, and so to see him suffer as a result of his bad call is all the more darkly ironic.

There’s no shortage of filmmakers who love Hitchcock, but the Coen brothers delivered an authentic throwback to 1949 thanks to their longstanding collaboration with the cinematographer Roger Deakins, who used shorter lenses and high contrast lighting to create the same sort of shadows that would appear in a classic noir. Setting the film at the tail end of the ‘40s, before the Cold War had sparked up, allowed the Coens to deal with a knotty period in American history when businesses were ramping up and ambitious investors were desperate to take advantage of new entrepreneurship opportunities, which may explain why Ed is so enthusiastic about the chance of escaping from the malaise of his empty life. This is where the title comes into play, because The Man Who Wasn’t There refers to more than just the unseen assailant in the crime who the police are seeking; it’s a reference to Ed himself, who has become so self-destructive that he’s nearly invisible to anyone around him.

The Man Who Wasn’t There features one of the Coens’ most underrated casts; Gandolfini in particular is excellent in a subversive role that asks him to be surprisingly vulnerable, despite still being an imposing physical presence. Although the film features an early performance from Scarlett Johansson which led to several other collaborations with the Coens, Thornton is downright brilliant as a tricky character who invites sympathy, despite the fact that his behavior is frequently destructive. Ed is in an sympathetic situation because he has been denied assistance for his alcoholism and cannot be blamed for his employment issues, and at first, his lack of passion would make him seem almost mild-mannered. It’s this transparency within his characterization that allows Thornton to truly shine, as it suggests that even those who have been emotionally deprived are capable of falling victim to human nature.

The Man Who Wasn’t There contains a number of fun Easter Eggs for cinephiles, but it is also a deeply strange film with one of the Coens’ most puzzling endings, which calls into question how much of the story should be taken seriously. In classic Hitchcock fashion, it’s a film that deals with suspenseful material while still being enormously entertaining. Although it didn’t get the attention that it deserved 25 years ago when it first debuted, The Man Who Wasn’t There is one of both the Coens’ and Thornton’s best films, as well as one that would make Hitchcock proud.

Honda Is Building A Racier Civic Type R For The U.S.

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Toyota has the new, rear-seat-less GRMN Corolla, Hyundai has its big-winged Elantra N TCR, and soon Honda will be bringing an even nuttier version of its Civic Type R to the party. On Friday, Honda confirmed that a Honda Racing Corporation version of its high-performance hatchback "is in development for the U.S. market." Translation: Honda is working on an even racier Civic Type R to sell in America. The company first showed off that HRC Concept at the Tokyo Auto Salon back in January, but it landed in North America for the first time at the SEMA Show in Las Vegas last week.

Not much is known about the HRC Civic, but judging from the red-and-purple-camo'd Concept, expect it to get more aggressive aero parts and cooling. If we had to guess based on how this sort of thing tends to go, we'd put our money on different, lighter wheels, stickier tires, tweaked suspension parts, some chassis stiffening, and maybe a modest power bump. Although we also wouldn't be that surprised if it doesn't come with any more power than the 315-hp standard model.

Honda says the CTR HRC is being made in the name of "ultimate pure sports performance" using HRC tech and racing driver expertise. A more extreme driving experience, then, is a given, but those specific words also read to us as "less compromising as a daily driver." Not unexpected or entirely a bad thing, given the premise, but probably not the Type R to get if you plan to, say, daily it year-round.

In any case, the HRC Concept CTR isn't the only car at Honda's SEMA stand this year, as it's joined by a Passport Baja trophy truck, a Passport TrailSport Baja Chase support vehicle, a Prelude modified by Hasport, and a handful of the automaker's race cars, including this year's Aston Martin AMR26 Formula 1 car, which uses a Honda RA626H power unit. Hopefully, the pumped-up Civic Type R that'll eventually hit U.S. showrooms will be a lot more competitive than that Aston.

AMD on track to overtake Intel as biggest x86 outfit

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

CEO says supply chain can cope with surging datacenter and AI demand

AMD has predicted it will win more revenue than Intel in its next quarter, potentially marking the first time it becomes the dominant vendor whose main product is x86 processors.

The house of Zen on Tuesday announced its Q3 2026 results, which saw it grow revenue 45 percent year-over-year to $13.4 billion. The company’s Data Center segment won $8.5 billion of revenue, up 97 percent year-over-year. Sales of silicon in AMD’s client and gaming segment slid eight percent to $3.7 billion, a worse fall than the five percent fall that helped to sink Intel’s share price.

Intel, AMD’s great rival in the x86 market, offered revenue guidance of $15 billion to $16 billion for its next quarter, which would represent almost no growth.

AMD, by contrast, told investors it expects around $17 billion of revenue next quarter.

CEO Lisa Su predicted even better days are ahead.

“We think 2027 is going to be a very strong momentum year for both the CPU business as well as the data center AI business,” she said on the company’s earnings call. “We have said the overall company will grow greater than 80 percent, and for the data center CPU business, we will about double, and for the data center AI business, it will significantly more than double.”

Su said AMD is confident its supply chain can meet that demand.

“We have worked across our supply chain to secure the wafers, substrates, advanced packaging, memory, and other key components needed to support much higher growth next year,” she said. “We are also deepening our collaboration with our supply chain partners and investing alongside them to secure the long-term capacity our growth will require.”

The CEO added that AMD has “strong confidence in our ability to sustain exceptional revenue growth for years to come, with earnings growing even faster.”

If those predictions are correct, AMD will likely accelerate away from Intel to become the leading provider of x86 processors after years of challenging Chipzilla's lead. Intel would still have higher overall revenue, due to its foundry operations, but in terms of revenue earned from selling chips, AMD would become the market leader.

AMD is arguably already the server CPU technology leader, and while it is second to Nvidia in the GPU market has trounced Intel.

Even AMD's declining client and gaming revenue has an upside because it was the gaming part of the portfolio that created the problem. PC chip sales rose seven percent year-over-year to $2.9 billion. Su said strong sales of Ryzen mobile processors drove that growth.

“Commercial is becoming a larger part of our client business as large enterprises adopt Ryzen PRO PCs across their employee base,” she said, pointing to strong adoption at the world’s largest banks, automotive companies, and healthcare concerns. Big corporate buyers are often a source of bigger profits and/or steadier revenue, so AMD looks to be taking the cream off the top of the PC market rather than scrounging for SMB and retail scraps.

Investors liked what they heard, sending AMD’s share price up by around five percent in after hours trading. ®

In Ohio, Amy Acton Defeats Vivek Ramaswamy for Governor

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Ohio was home to one of the nation’s most competitive races for governor in the 2026 midterm elections, as entrepreneur and former Republican presidential candidate Vivek Ramaswamy and Democrat physician Amy Acton battled to replace term-limited GOP governor Mike DeWine. But on Tuesday, Acton defeated Ramaswamy, according to the AP.

The Buckeye State, which is also home to Vice President JD Vance, hasn’t elected a Democrat to its highest office since 2006. But rising concerns regarding affordability and data centers boosted the campaign of Acton and running mate David Pepper, the former chair of the Ohio Democratic Party. Ramaswamy’s running mate, Rob McColley, is currently the Ohio Senate president.

A $4 million ad campaign from Ramaswamy had also split Republicans. The commercial featured Acton’s mother, from whom Acton has been estranged since her stepfather was reportedly arrested and charged with raping Acton when she was 12 years old. (According to The New York Times, the stepfather, who died in 2004, pleaded not guilty to the charges and then fled the state before trial.) In the ad, Acton’s 82-year-old mother, Donna Arthur, says, “Don’t let her become your governor,” prompting GOP members, including DeWine, to repudiate the commercial.

Meanwhile, Ohio’s US Senate race also went to the Democrats, further cementing their grip on the House. Over $500 million has been spent in the battle for the seat currently held by Republican senator Jon Husted, who was appointed to the role by DeWine in 2025 to replace JD Vance. Husted has been singled out for praise by President Donald Trump in recent weeks, especially when it comes to his physique, but trailed in the polls behind Democrat Sherrod Brown, who lost his previous seat in the US Senate to Republican Bernie Moreno in 2024. The Associated Press and CNN have projected Brown as the winner, giving his party a major win in one of the country's most closely watched Senate contests.

Kansas Elects Its First Democratic Senator in 94 Years

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Methodist minister Adam Hamiliton, a Democrat, has defeated Republican Roger Marshall in the race to represent Kansas in the U.S. Senate, ending a Democratic dry spell that has lasted nearly a century.

The mild-mannered megachurch pastor, who has been registered as a Republican, a Democrat, and an Independent over the course of his lifetime, assembled a broad coalition of voters in the heartland state, en route to pulling off one of the most improbable wins of the year in a state Donald Trump carried by over 16 percent just two years ago.

Marshall sought to paint his rival, the founding pastor of the largest united Methodist church in the country,  as “too woke” and “too weak” to represent Kansas, but voters appeared receptive to Hamiliton’s brand of scripture-laced centrism. “I’m pretty sure if Jesus were here [Marshall would] consider him too woke,” Hamilton said during his campaign.

In 2020, Marshall was re-elected with a margin of more than 11 points. This year, a surprisingly strong challenge from Hamilton, a difficult environment for Republicans, and new reporting about Marshall’s aggressive efforts to recoup payment from patients he treated when he was a practicing OB-GYN proved too much for the GOP nominee to overcome.

In polls throughout the election cycle, voters in Kansas consistently and overwhelmingly pointed to the economy as their top concern. In a state where nearly 90 percent of land is used for farming and ranching, the Republican candidate was damaged by the rising price of diesel gas — a side effect of the U.S. ongoing war with Iran — as well as the president’s decision to allow beef to be imported into the country at lower tariff rates.

Marshall, who was an OB-GYN before running for Senate, was hurt by the revelations that he had filed lawsuits against hundreds of patients who failed to pay their medical bills. The New York Times reported in September that at least 81 of Marshall’s patients were arrested for missing court dates related to their debts, including a pregnant woman with a 2-year-old daughter. That woman, Meischa Zimmerman, had been uninsured when Marshall delivered her child via a c-section in 2009; she was arrested three times as Marshall pursued payment. Talarico, Hinojosa win big in Texas despite Elon Musk’s millions

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

James Talarico, the charismatic Texas state representative who campaigned by talking extensively about his Christian faith, has defeated state Attorney General Ken Paxton, who was Donald Trump’s hand-picked choice, in Tuesday’s most high-profile U.S. Senate race. Late on Tuesday evening, Talarico led by more than a million votes, and the Associated Press called the race just before 11 p.m. Eastern time.

This marks the first time a Democrat has won a statewide race in Texas since 1994, and another such result, perhaps even more surprising, followed almost immediately. Democrat Gina Hinojosa has won the Texas governor’s race, according to the AP, beating Republican incumbent Gov. Greg Abbott by more than 700,000 votes.

The big blue wave of 2026 has featured numerous surprising results, including the upset victory by Democrat Annie Andrews over Republican Sen. Darline Graham in South Carolina’s Senate race. But these dramatic and unexpected Democratic wins in Texas stand out. The Senate campaign between Talarico and Paxton became one of the most expensive state-level races in American history, and according to CNN analyst David Axelrod, Hinojosa was outspent 10 to one by Abbott. Talarico’s campaign also became a major target for Democratic donors and an opportunity to vent their frustration and anger with Trump’s second presidential term.

A combined $573 million were spent by Talarico and Paxton, as well as their defeated primary opponents, over the course of this campaign. Much of that spending was the result of Elon Musk, the world’s richest man. The CEO of SpaceX and Tesla pumped an estimated $474 million into this race, virtually all of it to support Paxton, setting a new record for a single donor in a political campaign. Most of that money was funneled through went to Musk’s personal super PAC, but around $168 million went to the Senate Leadership Fund. Another $10 million went to the pro-Paxton Lone Star Liberty PAC, which spammed voters with AI-generated robocalls attacking Talarico. Meanwhile, Talarico’s campaign and Democratic PACs raised about $133 million.

Although Talarico, like other Democrats, focused on affordability and fighting corruption, he also sought to challenge the dominant view that only Republicans can be devout Christians. His campaign attracted various right-wing conspiracy theories, including claims that he was an emasculated “secret vegan” who suffered from mental illness. Democrats worried that this viral misinformation might overshadow numerous allegations about Paxton, including a Wall Street Journal report that he partied in Dubai with two fugitives from U.S. justice and a woman who wasn’t his wife.

Although Talarico has led in recent public polls, the size of his victory was unexpected. At this writing, it appears almost certain that Democrats will win the Senate, having already flipped seats in both South Carolina and North Carolina, where former Gov. Roy Cooper easily won the open seat held by outgoing Republican Sen. Thom Tillis. Democrats also lead the Senate races in Iowa, Maine and Ohio, although those races remain undecided.

All of those factors had won the GOP elections before, in different economic conditions. But this year, voters saw only the worst. Block Inc. shares rise 6% after-hours as it raises full-year profit forecast

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Block raised its full-year gross profit forecast to $12.63 billion, which would represent a 22% year-on-year growth.

Jack Dorsey-led fintech firm Block Inc. Block, Inc. (XYZ) saw its shares jump 6.2% in after-hours trading on Tuesday after reporting stronger third-quarter earnings and raising its full-year profit outlook.

Block posted a net income of $566 million in the third quarter of this year, up from $462 million a year earlier, according to its latest quarterly report released Tuesday. It generated $6.72 billion in total revenue, up from $6.12 billion in the same quarter last year.

The company's gross profit rose 22% year-on-year to $3.24 billion, while its Cash App booked $2.07 billion in gross profit, up 27% year-on-year.

On the back of the results, Block raised its full-year gross profit forecast to $12.63 billion, representing a 22% year-on-year growth.

"We are raising our full-year guidance to reflect the momentum we are seeing across Block," the company said. Block said it expects gross profit to grow at least 15% in 2027, as it continues to "expand margins while still investing meaningfully to sustain long-term growth."

Block shares closed down 1.4% at $76 on Tuesday but jumped 6.2% in after-hours trading following the earnings release.

Meanwhile, the firm's bitcoin ecosystem gross profit fell 50% year-over-year to $52 million in the third quarter, according to the report.

It attributed the decline to its "strategic decision to reduce the fee" charged on certain bitcoin transactions on Cash App. Block's bitcoin ecosystem primarily consists of customer purchases of bitcoin within Cash App, Proto, as well as bitcoin withdrawal fees, per the report.

Cash App generated $4.07 billion in revenue in Q3, including $1.77 billion from the bitcoin ecosystem segment.

HBO Max's 10-Episode Sci-Fi Series Could Be the Perfect 'Lanterns' Replacement

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Lanterns is the kind of success no one expected. The more grounded take on Green Lantern lore, combined with excellent character work from leads Kyle Chandler as Hal Jordan and Aaron Pierre as John Stewart, created a winning formula and made Lanterns one of the most-watched shows on HBO Max. While a second season of Lanterns remains in development, that doesn't mean DC Studios is done with Green Lantern. Pierre is set to return as John Stewart in Man of Tomorrow next year, while Nathan Fillion is reprising his role as Guy Gardner for Mister Miracle. Another series looks to embrace all the cosmic elements of Green Lantern while building out the world of another fan-favorite DC series.

Appropriately enough, this new series is titled My Adventures with Green Lantern. Spinning out of the events of My Adventures with Superman, My Adventures with Green Lantern will see Jessica Cruz (Auli'i Cravalho) joining the Green Lantern Corps. There's just one catch: while the Guardians of the Universe usually choose Green Lanterns, Jessica becomes a Green Lantern when a power ring literally lands in her lap. She's then pulled into the fight of her life "when more debris from the Lanterns’ ancient space war arrives – along with their alien foes," according to the official logline.

One of the major debates surrounding Lanterns is that its narrative felt better suited to a terrestrial DC hero like Batman or the Question than to two men charged with defending the universe from interstellar threats. While the show mentions other Green Lanterns like Ch'p the squirrel and Mogo the living planet, some viewers felt there could have been more Green Lantern elements in a show called Lanterns. My Adventures with Green Lantern is about to fulfill their wish, as the series is set to bring in plenty of characters from the comics. A preview at Annecy Festival reveals that Jessica attends Hal Jordan Memorial High, with her classmates, including future Green Lantern Kyle Rayner.

Jessica will also have to deal with other Green Lanterns, most notably the Corps' drill sergeant, Kilowog. She will also be fighting Yellow and Red Lanterns in the series, hinting at the presence of two iconic Green Lantern villains. The Yellow Lanterns are usually part of the Sinestro Corps, led by the titular Sinestro — Hal Jordan's former mentor and Green Lantern who now wields the yellow power of fear. The Red Lanterns, on the other hand, draw on the red power of rage thanks to their leader, Atrocitus. The presence of Yellow and Red Lanterns hints that My Adventures with Green Lantern will explore the emotional spectrum established in Geoff Johns' Green Lantern run, and that it'll be a more cosmic affair than Lanterns was. Producer Jake Wyatt also said that he wanted the villains to stand out at Annecy:

“We wanted to get weird with the bad guys. We needed hot evil people to scare the hell out of Jess and her buddies.”

Your answers point to the iconic sci-fi hero who shares your instincts, your values, and your particular way of facing the impossible.

You carry a weight most people would crumble under — the knowledge of what you're capable of, and the burden of what you might have to become.

You lead with instinct, warmth, and an absolute refusal to accept a no-win scenario — because you've always believed there's a third option nobody else has thought of yet.

You are the kind of person who holds the line when everyone else is losing faith — not because you're fearless, but because giving up simply isn't something you're capable of.

You are not reckless, not grandiose, and not particularly interested in being anyone's hero — you just refuse to stop when it matters.

You have been through fire that would break most people — and what came out the other side is something the world underestimates at its peril.

While My Adventures with Green Lantern is spinning out of My Adventures with Superman, it's inspired by another iconic series. My Adventures with Superman draws heavily on shonen anime like Dragon Ball Z, while My Adventures with Green Lantern draws heavily on Sailor Moon. A clip shown at Annecy features Jessica singing the iconic Green Lantern oath and transforming into a costume that largely resembles Sailor Moon's, putting another unique stamp on the Green Lantern mythos.

Lanterns was only the beginning of DC Studios fully embracing the Green Lantern comics, as My Adventures with Green Lantern looks poised to revamp the mythos for a new generation. It's a far cry from the days of Ryan Reynolds' box-office bomb, and the "brightest day" that these characters deserve.

Tennessee Voters Opt To Ban State-Level Property Taxes

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Voters in Tennessee are projected to approve Amendment 2, which bans any future statewide property tax and enshrines the prohibition in the state constitution.

Tennessee has not had a statewide property tax since 1949, though the state Legislature technically retained the constitutional authority to create one. Instead, property taxes are levied at the local level.

At 11 p.m. ET on Tuesday, the measure had 66.4% support with 82% of votes reporting, according to The Associated Press. An amendment to the Tennessee Constitution passes if it gets at least 50% support, and the number of “yes” votes is more than half of all votes cast for governor.

"It ensures that the state cannot impose property taxes again, putting taxpayers’ interests first and reinforcing local control," said Republican state Rep. Tandy Darby, who sponsored the amendment.

Amendment 2 would not prevent counties and municipal authorities from levying property taxes, but would prohibit the General Assembly from issuing property taxes now and in the future.

In order to change that, the state constitution would have to be amended once more, which would require a two-thirds vote in two legislative sessions and voter approval.

Republican Gov. Bill Lee came out in support of the initiative. It's supported by the National Federation of Independent Business as well as the Tennessee Realtors®.

Conservative nonprofit Tennessee Stands said the amendment "reinforces the principle that private property rights should never be subject to excessive government taxation or the threat of confiscation."

Some Democrats have opposed it on the grounds that the ban does not need to be written into the constitution and should remain a state statute.

She was also a senior editor at BuzzFeed, covering lifestyle, fashion, and beauty. Xavier Becerra Declared Winner of California Governor’s Race

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Xavier Becerra has been declared the winner of the California governor’s race, easily swatting away a challenge from Republican Steve Hilton.

Becerra, the Democrat, ran on a platform of lowering energy costs and working toward universal health care. The dominant theme of his campaign was opposition to President Trump, noting that he had sued the president more than 100 times as attorney general during Trump’s first term. Related Stories ‘Father’s House,’ Starring David Dencik and Trine-Dyrholm, Picked Up by DR Sales Ahead of Tallinn Competition

After emerging as the top vote-getter in the June primary, Becerra had an easy path to victory over Hilton. He has made relatively few firm commitments — including on the issue of expanding California’s film and TV tax incentive.

“Everyone recognizes that one of the reasons Southern California is such a destination point for tourists and people around the world is because of things like Hollywood, because of Universal Studios, Disneyland,” Becerra told Variety last month. “And so we have to fight to keep that industry here in Southern California.”

Gov. Gavin Newsom announced a proposal on Monday to expand the state’s $750 million production tax credit to $1 billion, to include above-the-line costs, and to extend the sunset date to 2040. But Newsom will be out of office in two months, leaving any consideration of expanding the incentive to his successor.

Many in Hollywood are pushing for an unlimited tax incentive, which would function as an entitlement and would not require discretionary approval from the state film commission. An Assembly special committee held a hearing on that issue last week, but the chair, Rick Chavez Zbur, said afterward that the idea of an unlimited incentive is a “heavy lift.”

Becerra has not taken a position on the issue, saying only that he wants to work to enhance the credit.

“I’ll sit down with all the legislators to see what kind of proposals are put before us,” he told Variety. “But we’re going to fight.”

Becerra’s campaign did not respond to a request for comment on Newsom’s proposal on Monday. Becerra posted a film policy on his website that did not lay out explicit commitments and that was rated by Pangram as 100% AI-generated.

Becerra served in Congress before being appointed to replace Kamala Harris as the state attorney general. In the Biden administration, he served as secretary of the Department Health and Human Services. He emerged from a crowded primary field last spring, becoming the frontrunner only after Rep. Eric Swalwell was forced to withdraw and resign from Congress amid sexual misconduct allegations.

His strongest Democratic opponent was hedge fund billionaire Tom Steyer, who had the backing of IATSE and a slew of progressive groups.

Texas Goes Blue: James Talarico Triumphs Over Ken Paxton

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The myth of a blue Texas is now a reality.

Democrat James Talarico has defeated Republican state Attorney General Ken Paxton in one of the most hotly contested Senate races of the year. Shortly after Talarico was projected to win, Democrat Gina Hinojosa was projected the winner over longtime Republican Governor Greg Abbott.

The victories marks a seismic shift in Texas politics, and in the nation’s current political map, ending a 32-year statewide drought for Democrats in the Republican stronghold. The party now controls the state’s governership, as well as a U.S. Senate seat there for at least the next six years.

The Senate race started with two bitter primaries, with Talarico defeating Rep. Jasmine Crockett (D-Texas) on the Democratic side, and Paxton defeating incumbent Senator John Cornyn after landing Donald Trump’s endorsement. Cornyn refused to endorse his party’s nominee after losing the primary, citing Paxton’s long record of corruption and abuse of office. In audio leaked to Rolling Stone last month, Paxton griped about how much the contentious primary with Paxton hurt his chances.

Cornyn was one of the first to celebrate Talarico’s win on Tuesday. “The voters have spoken, and I want to extend my congratulations to James Talarico on his election to the U.S. Senate,” he wrote. “I look forward to meeting him and offering my assistance with his transition as he prepares to serve Texans in the U.S. Senate.”

Throughout most of the general election campaign, Talarico held a polling lead over Paxton. The upstart candidate barnstormed the state for months before Paxton even began to put on his campaigning shoes. Talarico, a state House member, ran on an economically progressive platform focused on cost of living issues and anti-corruption. Paxton, a poster boy for corruption, ran largely on his allegiance to Trump while trying to paint Talarico as a radical leftist.

Talarico sought to take advantage of a uniquely abysmal landscape for Republicans across the country to steal a seat for Democrats in a state that voted for Trump by a 13-point margin in 2024. Trump, ironically enough, turned out to be Talarico’s biggest campaign asset. The president’s approval numbers tanked when he launched his war against Iran this spring — breaking his “no more wars” campaign promise and sending the price of fuel skyrocketing. It hasn’t exactly been a winning strategy. Inflation is up, wage growth is stagnant, and Americans can’t afford the life their parents had. At the same time, Elon Musk, the world’s first trillionaire dumped almost half a billion dollars into Republican reelection efforts — much of it into Texas.Paxton, like many Texas Republicans, spent the campaign operating under the assumption that just being the GOP nominee would be enough to notch a win. Trump’s support of Paxton — over an incumbent Republican and the objections of Senate GOP leaders —  elevated the Texas race into a referendum on Trumpism writ large. The president campaigned throughout the state alongside Paxton, including hosting an “emergency” midterm republican convention in Dallas, Texas. Paxton was then caught complaining in leaked audio that the president’s appearance had hurt his numbers. Unable to distance himself from the president, Paxton and his allies attempted to reframe the race towards culture war fear mongering. Despite the monetary advantages held by the GOP, Trump has shed basically all the support he managed to accumulate among disaffected Hispanic voters during the 2024 election — a critical constituency within Texas. Early voting data indicated that enthusiasm among Republican voters was trailing Democrats.

The candidates spent the final hours of the campaign making last-second pitches to voters, offering vastly competing visions for the state and nation.

At a Monday concert rally in the heart of Austin, Talarico and Hinojosa spoke before a full house crowd of almost 2,000 people. Talarico, swimming against a three-decade long tide of GOP rule in the state, reminded his supporters of the Texas of yore — and the Democrats who shaped it.

“We are the state that pioneered ranching and energy and computers. We are the state that gave this country Barbara Jordan, Anne Richards, LBJ and the great society,” Talarico said. “We are the state that put breakfast in a taco. There is nothing we can’t do.”

The Democrat contrasted his vision of the state with the governance of state and federal Republican leadership. “We have an affordability crisis because we have a corruption crisis,” Talarico said, repeatedly referencing Ken Paxton’s extensive record of profiteering and abuse of office. “This election should be about chasing a vision of what this state can be. Texas public schools that are the envy of the nation. A Texas economy that is second to none and Texas families that are stronger and healthier than they have ever been before. This won’t happen overnight, but a giant state deserves giant dreams.”

Paxton was originally scheduled to close his “Come and Take It” campaign tour with a large rally at The Alamo — a historic landmark of the Texas Revolution famously taken by the Mexican force. Paxton cancelled the rally and instead shifted his event to Allen, Texas, in the suburbs of Dallas.

The state attorney general’s closing pitch to voters was decidedly less rosy. In front of a much smaller crowd, Paxton warned of the threats of socialism, transgender inclusive healthcare, men in women’s sports, unfettered mass immigration, and a whole host of culture war priorities that had so far failed to secure him a major lead in the polls.  Trending Stories Trump Is Already Crying That He’s Not to Blame for Republican Wipeout Elizabeth Holmes' Partner Did an AMA About That Shower Scene, His Sniffing, and 'You Can See Everything' Democrats Win Total Control of Congress Mick Jagger Praises Sam Fender’s Rolling Stones Cover

“We are all sinners and we all need you,” Paxton told supporters in a prayer ahead of his speech.

In the end, not enough Texans bought Paxton’s pitch.

7 Old-Fashioned Tools for the Modern-Day Gardener

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

All Gardenista stories—from garden tours and expert advice to hand tools and furniture roundups.

Starting from scratch or upgrading an outdoor space? Look here for advice on plants and hardscape materials. We have 200 guides on everything from fences to foxgloves.

Heading somewhere? Plan your trip with our destination guides to our favorite public gardens, hotels, restaurants, and shops.

Gardenista’s members-only directory of landscape architects and garden designers.

See our obsessively curated catalog of favorite products sourced by the Gardenista editors.

Everything old is new again. Recently, we’ve been researching gardening tools from older generations that have stood the test of time. Here are five that intrigue us:

This unglazed terracotta pot is an ancient watering tool that is buried beneath the soil, where the water slowly releases itself through the pot’s pores to your plant’s roots. Clay pot irrigation reduces runoff, evaporation, and your water bill.

Invented in 1913, this best-selling weeder uses leverage to make weeding easier; it also eliminates bending over and having to yank pesky garden invaders by hand. What you do is put the steel gripping prongs over the weed, push the tool into the ground and then move the wooden handle toward the foot pad lever. The weed pops out, root and all.

This tool was first recorded in Roman times and has been used by farmers for planting crops ever since. It’s sharp wooden point pokes into the ground to create a hole into which you can drop seeds, seedlings, or bulbs.

Used for generations because it is easy to push and move around the garden, this plow and cultivator will make simple work of preparing beds, weeding between rows, and breaking up dry soil. Not recommended for rocky or clay soil.

“Once, lawns were cut with scythes and the results weren’t bad; Versailles for instance was cut that way,” begins our story on using these ancient tools instead of ear-splitting modern-day lawn mowers.

Used for centuries by some Native Americans, the idea is simple: changes in barometric pressure cause the stick to move. The stick curves up when clear skies are nearing and the stick bends down when either snow or rain are on the way.

ANC faces anger over poor services as South Africans vote in local elections

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Party expected to get less than a third of the vote amid frustration over water outages and power cuts

The African National Congress is on course for a bruising result in South Africa’s local elections on Wednesday as voters go to the polls amid deep discontent over water outages, power cuts and crumbling infrastructure.

The former liberation movement, which Nelson Mandela led to power in 1994 at the end of apartheid as South Africa’s first black president, is still likely to be the largest party. However, polls show it getting less than a third of the vote, down from 45.6% at the last municipal elections in 2021 and 40.2% in 2024’s national elections.

Vote fragmentation means the ANC could still end up leading many city coalition governments while losing control of others.

Unstable coalitions are already a feature of many of South Africa’s 257 municipalities, and analysts point to their high turnover as a major reason for poor service delivery.

Johannesburg, a city of 4.8 million people that has faced water shortages for more than a week, has had 10 mayors in as many years. The neighbouring municipality of Ekurhuleni has had three mayors since 2021. To the north, in Tshwane, which contains the capital, Pretoria, the mayoralty has rotated between parties four times since 2020. In October, more than 800 people contracted a gastrointestinal illness from contaminated water in Bronkhorstspruit, in east Tshwane.

Johannesburg, Ekurhuleni and Tshwane are in Gauteng, South Africa’s most populous and densely populated province and its economic engine. The centre-right, white-led Democratic Alliance is leading the polls in all three metros but has not been polling above 40%.

The ANC’s national leadership is open to working with the DA in Gauteng, according to South African media, mirroring the government of national unity, where the two parties are the largest in the national coalition. However, local ANC leaders are reportedly not in favour of a DA tie-up. Currently, the ANC governs with a combination of the far-left Economic Freedom Fighters and the populist Patriotic Alliance and ActionSA across the province’s three metros.

In Johannesburg, the DA’s Helen Zille has led a spirited campaign, with viral stunts including the 75-year-old snorkelling in a pothole and kayaking along a flooded street. However, the former Cape Town mayor is still viewed with suspicion by many, especially black voters, due to controversial past comments appearing to excuse colonialism. Some voters also point to soaring housing costs in Cape Town, which has been run by the DA since 2006, as a reason not to vote for Zille.

The former Johannesburg DA mayor Herman Mashaba, who leads ActionSA, which is polling around 10%, has ruled out working with Zille in Johannesburg. Mashaba has called Zille a “racist”, an allegation she has always fervently denied. The ANC, for its part, has three possible mayoral candidates for the city, amid factional infighting.

In eThekwini metro, which contains the port city of Durban, the uMkhonto we Sizwe (MK) party of the former ANC president Jacob Zuma is polling above 40%. The 84-year-old Zuma, who has weathered and denied numerous corruption allegations, is set to take many of the votes of his former party, which won 42% in eThekwini in 2021 and is now polling around 10%.

The DA looks set to retain its majority in Cape Town, although probably with less than the 58% of the vote it won in 2021, as unaffordable housing turns more voters against the party.

South Africa’s electoral commission will have a week to announce the final results of Wednesday’s vote, which chooses councillors and parties, with mayors not directly elected. Parties will then have two weeks from the announcement to form councils and elect mayors.

GTA 5’s Unsolvable Mystery Broke Fans for Over a Decade, and GTA 6 Should Do It Again

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Mount Chiliad spawned thousands of theories

In Grand Theft Auto 5, one landmark was visible practically no matter where you were in San Andreas: Mount Chiliad. "Claimed by the Spanish in 1769, Mount Chiliad is the highest peak in San Andreas, sitting at a height of 2,744 meters," reads a sign at the nearby Silent Probe Mountain art installation. "Taken under Mexican control in 1821, it was then, and remains in American hands since 1847."

It's more than just a mountain, though. Once the player has achieved 100% story completion, standing atop the mountain at 3 a.m. during cloudy weather reveals a UFO, hovering in the night sky. It's marked with an F.I.B. logo, aka the Federal Investigation Bureau, implying that it was created by the government. However, there's also reason to believe that the mysterious aircraft is a hoax, because it doesn't react to bullets, and raindrops nearby become pixelated.

The running theory is that it's a hologram, as opposed to the two other functional UFOs in the game, which are physical objects. They're located high in the sky above both Fort Zancudo and the Silent Probe Mountain respectively, they can both be collided with, and they emit electromagnetic pulses that cause the player's vehicle to shut down if they get too close. There's reason to believe they're both also government-controlled: the former visually looks somewhat similar to a real-life B-2 Spirit stealth bomber and, when viewed using third-party software, has two human pilots inside. The latter also has F.I.B. printed on the side, much like the UFO above Mt. Chiliad.

Players knew there was at least one secret at the top of the mountain, thanks to graffiti on the back of the observation deck that reads "come back when your story is complete." GTA Online revealed that the mountain is also home to a nuclear launch facility. But some players are convinced that Mt. Chiliad holds one final mystery that is still unsolved to this day.

Inside the tramway station near the summit, a mural depicts an abstract interpretation of the mountain itself. At the top is an eye, while the exterior of the mountain has lightning bolts emanating from it. Inside the mountain is a maze of perpendicular connecting lines. Each one connects to one of eight squares. Five of them are small, marked with an X inside. The three larger ones at the bottom of the picture show, from left to right, a UFO, a cracked egg, and a person wearing a jetpack.

One week after GTA 5 was released, on Sept. 24, 2013, fans created the Chiliad Mystery subreddit. For the last 13 years, users there have been dedicated to uncovering the secrets believed to be held within. The community worked out that the X's correspond to petroglyphs hidden around the mountain, each of which depicts a weather status required for the Mt. Chiliad UFO to appear. There's also a theory that each X also represents the roman numeral for 10, and since there are five of them, it's representative of the 50 collectible spaceship (aka UFO) parts scattered across San Andreas.

In the GTA Online mission involving the nuclear facility, the player escapes by using a jetpack. Is that what the jetpack drawing on the mural is referring to? It’s one of the mural’s lingering riddles. It's certainly plausible, if a little underwhelming, because the common belief was that unraveling the mystery would result in a permanent jetpack vehicle, usable in GTA 5’s single-player mode, not exclusive to Online. However, some think the jetpack just refers to the UFO above Fort Zancudo, because in GTA: San Andreas, the jetpack is found at the military base.

Then there's the cracked egg. This has been the biggest source of confusion for the sleuths working on the mystery, and there are a number of theories. It could refer to the Epsilon cult, as they believe the world hatched from an egg. It could also tie in to the fountain by the Maze Bank Tower in Los Santos, as the two bear a close resemblance. Or there's the most common theory, which is that it is simply a hint to the underwater UFO found in Paleto Bay, on the northern coast of San Andreas.

So with all of that in mind, there's every chance that the mural is simply one giant hint to finding each of the UFOs in GTA 5. But believing that would also mean believing that the Chiliad mystery is solved, and there's no fun in that, because it would be a seriously disappointing outcome. And for the users on the subreddit, that isn't even a possibility.

There are hundreds of loose ends and theories players have discovered. Even in the last year, they’ve posted dozens of new ideas. Here's user Alexisuwuu23, who is convinced an in-game film, "Capolavoro," holds the secret because a billboard ad for it can be found at the location where streetlights form a question mark if looked at from the observatory, and it all ties into a disappearing ladder. "Intentional, obviously," reads one reply. "Gotta be a marker or something. The hunt continues."

In September, AntelopeIcy6558 posed a theory about the eight lights on top of the Maze Bank Tower helipad. "I believe that to solve the secret, you need to shoot these eight lights in a certain order," the post reads. "Why? I believe there are eight locations around Los Santos that we need to find. Each of these locations from some angle resembles a keyhole, which is one of the main symbols of this mystery."

There are other theories that think the mystery stretches beyond GTA 5, and also includes Red Dead Redemption 2. Cancelled DLCs and datamined files have revealed a vast array of potential clues, but since those projects were never officially released, they're unlikely to ever provide a definitive answer. Then there are those who predict GTA 6 will provide a resolution.

Twelve years ago, user talkol made a post titled "pretty solid proof that the game was released without a jetpack or flyable UFO," which outlined what is still the most damning evidence we've had: there is nothing hinting at either of those vehicles in the game's files. Of course, that didn't dissuade the masses, because posts like these have been non-stop for the last 13 years.

Last year, as reported on by IGN, YouTuber Gator Keys uploaded a video titled "This is The End of the Mount Chiliad Mystery." He kicks it off by stating what many realists would agree with: "I'm not claiming I'm the first person to come to this conclusion. I'm sure many other players did in 2013, but for some reason, the Mt. Chiliad mystery remains in 2025. So there needs to be a video that puts an end to it before GTA 6 releases."

Gator Keys goes into plenty of detail in the eight-minute-long video, but to sum it up, his explanation is that the mystery is nowhere near as deep as the community had believed. The answer is essentially that the government is all-seeing and all-powerful, as represented by the F.I.B. logos on the UFOs and the eye on top of the mural. It's simply a satirical take on real life, similar to the idea of the Illuminati.

Ultimately, the "true" answer for the Chiliad mystery is irrelevant. The mystery itself spawned a thousands-strong community, with everyone cooperating and theorizing. It's reminiscent of when that kid at school promised they'd found Mew underneath a truck in Pokémon Red and Blue. Easter eggs and gaming mysteries these days are usually solved within days, thanks to datamining and the sharing of information via the internet. GTA 5, on the other hand, whether intentionally or not, gave players something to talk about for 13 years.

A weekly cheat sheet to what you’ll eventually love to play. No buying, no commitment — just saving for later.

There's no doubt GTA 6 will spawn plenty more theories and Easter eggs, whether they're a continuation of the Chiliad mystery or something entirely new. It'll also likely be the biggest video game of all time, whether it includes a decade-long mystery or not. But the longevity of the Chiliad mystery makes one thing clear: Rockstar is the champion of this type of lore, and including something similar, where the game never truly feels complete without it being solved, will keep a huge portion of the community occupied for years to come.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

The best of Polygon.com, directly in your inbox.

Most Venture Capital Is Going To Companies That Are Founded Or Led By Billionaires

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The majority of U.S. venture capital nowadays goes to companies founded or led by men who are already billionaires.

This is not a difficult stat to unearth. So far this year, just seven companies raised more than half of all seed- through growth-stage investment. They’re mostly familiar names: OpenAI, Anthropic, xAI (now part of SpaceX), Anduril Industries, Databricks, Prometheus and The Boring Co.

These companies raised more than $260 billion in equity funding in the first 10 months of the year. That’s just over 50% of the U.S. seed and venture investment over the same period, per Crunchbase data.

Founders and CEOs of these seven companies are also well-known — and exceptionally  well-off to boot.

Two founders are particularly famous. They are the world’s wealthiest men: Elon Musk and Jeff Bezos.

Musk founded xAI and The Boring Co., and also co-founded OpenAI, (though he has no known equity stake in the latter). And while outside investors funded these companies, Musk could have also afforded to do so himself.

Ditto for Bezos, who in 2025 launched the physical AI startup Prometheus, which raised $12 billion earlier this year. Bezos’ own net worth, meanwhile, is calculated at more than $350 billion.

The two highest valuation startups — OpenAI and the soon-to-be-public Anthropic — also have prominent billionaire founders and CEOs. However, they have mixed backstories to their wealth.

OpenAI co-founder and CEO Sam Altman primarily made his vast wealth through investments in Y Combinator startups, including Stripe and Reddit. Altman has said he does not have an equity stake in OpenAI beyond a sliver from an old YC round. Co-founder Greg Brockman, by contrast, has a larger stake in OpenAI that was reportedly valued around $30 billion earlier this year.

At Anthropic, meanwhile, all seven company co-founders have similar-sized equity stakes, and each is worth billions. This includes the AI giant’s most famous co-founder, CEO Dario Amodei.

Databricks and Anduril, meanwhile, both have founders serving as CEOs who have amassed billion-dollar-plus net worths during their tenures.

Databricks co-founder and CEO Ali Ghodsi has a reported $7.6 billion net worth, with the company he leads valued at around $190 billion following an August financing.

Anduril co-founder and CEO Brian Schimpf, meanwhile, has an estimated $1.5 billion net worth as a result of his stake in the $61 billion valuation defense industry disruptor. Co-founder Palmer Luckey, who sold his virtual reality startup Oculus to Meta for $2 billion in 2014 before starting Anduril, has an estimated $5.9 billion net worth.

Several other founders and CEOs of startups that raised some of this year’s largest rounds are billionaires too. This includes Wonder founder Marc Lore, Figure founder Brett Adcock, and Surge AI founder and CEO Edwin Chen, among others.

We can split the billionaires who founded this year’s most heavily funded startups into two broad subcategories. There are those who became billionaires through the startup that just raised capital. And there are those who were already billionaires through prior companies or investments.

Ghodsi, Amodei and Brockman fit in the first group. In the second group, of course, we can put Musk and Bezos, as well as Altman and Luckey, who became a 21-year-old billionaire following the Oculus sale.

Historically, neither billionaire type was a particularly common recipient of venture funding rounds. The term “unicorn” as a moniker for private companies valued at $1 billion or more only entered the lexicon in 2013, when this asset class became noticeably large. In prior decades, founders and CEOs mostly only hit billionaire status after their startups went public or sold in high-priced acquisitions.

But times change. And in tandem, our image of what a startup funding recipient looks like should evolve as well.

True, quite a bit of capital still flows to the stereotypical venture recipients: first-time founders or serial entrepreneurs whose prior companies didn’t make them fabulously wealthy. But a majority of venture funding now does not go to these classic startup types.

Instead, it goes to companies with valuations already high enough to warrant billionaire status for their stakeholders as well as ventures founded by men who are already billionaires.

Stay up to date with recent funding rounds, acquisitions, and more with the Crunchbase Daily.

Luann de Lesseps Reveals Where She Stands With Bethenny Frankel

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

It's been way longer than a New York minute since Luann de Lesseps and Bethenny Frankel were Big Apple BFFs.

Over eight years after the former RHONY costars last shared the screen, Luann gave a rare update on where things stand between her and the Skinnygirl founder.

"Bethenny and I will always share the history of Housewives," The Golden Life star exclusively told hosts Eyal Booker and Lonnie Marts IIV on E!’s Hot Goss: The Golden Life Edition. "I don't see her that often."

While the Countess didn't specify whether the two remain on cool—or uncool—terms, Luann had an even more blunt response when Bethenny's name popped up during the E! reality series' Nov. 3 episode.

As Luann quipped in a confessional when producers asked for an update on their friendship: "I'm good with that."

But just as friends come and go in life, so do tough times. Which is why Luann has no problem mentioning it all—including reflecting on lessons she's learned from her 2017 arrest—on The Golden Life.

"I'm honest about my life and I share my experience," the 61-year-old—mom to daughter Victoria, 31, and son Noel, 27, with ex Alexandre de Lesseps—explained to E!. "I hope it inspires other people to live their truth. I just do what feels good to me and, at this point in my life, I'm too old to not live my reality."

Today, Luann's reality is full of love thanks to boyfriend of three years Michael Riemerschmid. The two remain happily together, despite Sonja Morgan shading Michael on The Golden Life by claiming he's still married to his ex.

The "Money Can’t Buy You Class" singer is even taking that in stride, pointing to BFF Dorinda Medley’s words to live by.

"He's not married," Luann clarified on Hot Goss. "What did Dorinda say? She lies about all kinds of s--t. She was lying about that too."

Plus, as the former Housewife elaborated, "When I met Michael, he was separated. So, he was not divorced. Since Sonja's known him, he's been divorced. He's not a married man anymore."

As for why Luann thinks Sonja was bad-mouthing her man?

"She likes to live in the past a little bit," the cabaret star quipped. "She's still in the '90s."

Follow Luann's life in Palm Beach when The Golden Life airs Tuesdays at 10 p.m. on E! followed by Hot Goss' exclusive interviews with the cast each week. In the meantime, keep reading to get better acquainted with the rest of The Golden Life cast.

An OG castmember of The Real Housewives of New York City, Ramona Singer first burst onto our televisions in 2008 and has been a reality TV meme queen ever since.

Known for her love of pinot grigio (a.k.a. Turtle Time!) and outspoken, unfiltered personality, the former businesswoman and entrepreneur recently relocated from NYC to Palm Beach, Fla., where she spends much of her time attending charity events in addition to spending quality time with 31-year-old daughter Avery, whom she shares with ex-husband Mario Singer.

Dorinda Medley worked in fashion in New York and London before meeting her first husband Ralph Lynch in the '90s. The two welcomed daughter Hannah, now 33, in 1993 before breaking up.

The New England native married her second husband hedge fund advisor Richard Medley in 2005 and he passed away six years later.

After years working the Big Apple social scene, Dorinda joined RHONY in 2015 and frequently hosted castmembers at her grand Berkshires estate Bluestone Manor.

While starring on the Bravo series for five years prior to her 2020 departure, Dorinda coined many iconic phrases, including, "I made it nice," "How am I doing? Not well, bitch!" and "Clip! Clip!"

A New York native, Sonja Morgan began her career in fashion and hospitality in the '90s before becoming a full-time housewife to ex-husband John Adams Morgan, a member of the elite Morgan family.

Prior to their 2006 divorce, the couple welcomed daughter Quincy, now 26.

In 2010, Sonja joined RHONY as the self-proclaimed "straw that stirs the drink," becoming known for countless scene-stealing moments over the years.

After selling her Manhattan townhouse in 2024, Sonja is ready for a fresh start in the Sunshine State.

Like Ramona, Luann de Lesseps helped usher in the legacy of Big Apple Housewives, starring on the series for over a decade from season one until her season 13 departure.

Formerly known as "the Countess" while married to ex Count Alexandre de Lesseps, she and the French aristocrat—who divorced in 2009 after 16 years of marriage—share two kids: Daughter Victoria, 32, and son Noel, 30.

Luann, who was also briefly married to ex Tom D'Agostino from 2016 to 2017, has since become an internationally renowned cabaret star, touring the world with her hit songs including "Money Can't Buy You Class" and "Chic C'est La Vie."

Kelly Bensimon rose to fame as a model in the early '00s, posing for top designers and meeting ex-husband Gilles Bensimon in the process. The two, who split in 2007, are parents to daughters Sea, 28, and Teddy, 26.

The Illinois native transitioned from the fashion world to reality TV in 2009 when she joined The Real Housewives of New York City as a main castmember.

In recent years, Kelly has made a name for herself as a luxury realtor.

The Dangers of a Lame-Duck Trump

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

DANIEL W. DREZNER is Academic Dean and Distinguished Professor of International Politics at the Fletcher School of Law and Diplomacy at Tufts University and the author of the newsletter Drezner’s World.

ELIZABETH N. SAUNDERS is Professor of Political Science at Columbia University, a Nonresident Senior Fellow at the Brookings Institution, and the author of The Insiders’ Game: How Elites Make War and Peace.

How Far Will He Go to Make Peace—and War?

Daniel W. Drezner and Elizabeth N. Saunders

With the midterms over, U.S. President Donald Trump is now a lame duck. Second-term presidents who lose control of Congress have to face the reality that the time for signature legislation or other domestic policy initiatives has passed. As they think about their legacy, lame ducks often turn to foreign policy, where the power of the presidency allows them to act unfettered by pesky domestic politics. It is not unusual for a lame duck to reach for a final, lofty goal abroad. U.S. President Bill Clinton, for instance, put great effort into trying to advance a proposal for a final settlement to the Israeli-Palestinian conflict at the end of his presidency.

Trump’s lame-duck period will not be like those of his predecessors, however. He has already gone through what was widely considered at the time to be a lame-duck phase, between November 2020 and January 2021. He used it to contest the election outcome despite clear evidence that Joe Biden had won. Trump is the first president in the past 100 years to have served two nonconsecutive terms, and therefore the only American leader in living memory to have a second lame-duck period—this time lasting more than two years.

With Democrats soon to be in control of the House and the Senate, the threat of impeachment or investigations into the Trump family’s business dealings will likely make the president feel cornered and angry. And that’s what makes the remainder of Trump’s term so dangerous. A boxed-in Trump, with no obvious constructive foreign policy outlet to channel his energy, may well lash out internationally. And Congress, even if controlled by Democrats, will have little power over his day-to-day management of foreign policy.

There are plenty of ways Trump could act out: issuing fresh threats against Canada, launching new attacks on U.S. alliances, or colluding with Russia or China at the expense of Ukraine or Taiwan. But to turn those threats into policy would require serious effort, such as repositioning U.S. troops or painstakingly negotiating with other great powers.

The two places where Trump can inflict the most damage are Iran and Cuba. According to The New York Times, the Trump administration and Israel have drawn up plans for fresh strikes on Iran as soon as the midterms are over. A last-ditch effort to compel Tehran to surrender is unlikely to work and would put at risk the global economy and the security and stability of Persian Gulf allies. Trump might also try to force regime change in Cuba. Military action against both adversaries would only require Trump to give his say-so. With no election on the horizon, all that constrains the president abroad is the sense of his own legacy.

We define presidents as lame-duck when they no longer need to contend with elections, either presidential or midterm. For a one-term president who has lost a reelection bid, this period lasts just a few months, between their successor’s election and inauguration. For a second-term president, it can last two years, between the midterms and when they leave office. (Trump, who has never been particularly interested in the political fortunes of other Republicans, is unlikely to let the 2028 presidential election affect his thinking.)

Freed from the burdens of campaigning and legislating, and often lacking domestic political capital, lame-duck leaders tend to pivot to diplomacy, taking chances abroad that they would not have dared to before. Clinton tried to broker an Israeli-Palestinian peace agreement and arrange a presidential trip to North Korea at the end of his second term. After losing the 2014 midterms, President Barack Obama concluded the Iran nuclear deal and normalized relations with Cuba.

Lame-duck presidents have also used military force in ways they might not have done in the lead-up to elections. George H. W. Bush, for example, intervened in Somalia in late 1992 with a limited mission to ease the famine. More than a decade later, in 2007, George W. Bush sent a surge of forces to stabilize Iraq after a punishing midterm election in which voters seemed to send the message that he should withdraw from the failing war there.

The results of these foreign policy moonshots have been mixed. Clinton’s Israeli-Palestinian negotiations failed; Obama’s diplomatic wins were short-lived; George H. W. Bush’s Somalia mission was largely successful in staving off hunger but left the roots of the conflict unresolved, tempting Clinton to expand the mission with disastrous consequences; and George W. Bush’s Iraq surge quelled violence in the short term but did little to permanently stabilize the country. These endeavors were, at the very least, debated and planned with input from relevant parts of government and outside experts. Their aims were clear, even if unmet. And in each case the president attempted to explain and justify his policies—standards Trump has routinely failed to meet.

With Trump now diminished by the midterms yet still on a quest to win a Nobel Peace Prize, one might expect him to turn to diplomacy. The problem is that he has already exhausted many of the obvious lame-duck gambits. During his first term, Trump swung for the foreign policy fences by pulling out of the 2015 Iran nuclear deal, meeting North Korean leader Kim Jong Un three times, replacing the North American Free Trade Agreement with his own regional trade pact, facilitating the normalization of ties between Israel and a handful of Arab countries, and withdrawing from a raft of international agreements. Since returning to office in 2025, he has presided over a cease-fire in Gaza and the capture of Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro.

Trump is not expected to return to most of these initiatives because he has reached the limits of what his spectacle-driven diplomacy can achieve. Trump vowed to take Greenland, for example, but has settled for a deal that expands the U.S. military presence in the territory. His “Board of Peace,” which he pitched as a rival to the United Nations Security Council, will continue to languish as other countries now feel less need to kowtow to his every whim.

There are other long shots Trump could attempt, such as trying to end intractable conflicts in Kashmir or Sudan, to forge a reputation as a peacemaker. But these are exceptionally difficult problems to solve in the best of circumstances. Even forcing a lopsided deal in these cases would require a level of deftness, regional expertise, and patience that Trump lacks. He rarely invests meaningful time in diplomacy, as illustrated by his dubious claims to have ended a slew of conflicts, many of which are still ongoing.

Perhaps the biggest swing Trump could take would be to fashion a great-power concert—a forum in which he, Russian President Vladimir Putin, and Chinese leader Xi Jinping worked together to impose global order. As with most of his prior meetings with Putin and Xi, however, future summits are unlikely to produce anything of substance. Neither Russia nor China has expressed much interest in the trilateral nuclear arms treaty that Trump has floated. Nor are they keen on pressing Iran to acquiesce to a deal with the United States.

Trump could use those meetings to throw Ukraine or Taiwan under the bus in the desperate hope of a peace deal. But as the past two years have demonstrated, Putin is not interested in compromise and has the resources to keep fighting. Xi views Taiwan as a domestic Chinese matter and will be unlikely to accept any constraints on his approach to the island. Trump will eventually figure out that he’s better off capitalizing on the optics of a summit of the three leaders than putting in the work to reach any kind of agreement.

Trump is most likely to take action in Iran. He seems to believe that escalating the war could break the stalemate and rescue his legacy. But Iranian leaders won’t back down in the face of more force. Instead, they will probably ramp up attacks on commercial shipping, strike Gulf allies, and ignore diplomatic overtures until Trump leaves office. Moreover, Trump’s own options may be limited: a more radical U.S. escalation against Iran, such as deploying troops to Kharg Island, would probably be so unpopular within his base that he would avoid it. Even Trump’s biggest supporters are growing tired of the high prices that his war has caused. Renewed conflict could plunge the world into economic crisis.

Trump could also attempt to force regime change in Cuba. He has imposed a full-blown embargo on the island for most of 2026 in an effort to weaken its defenses. Cut off from its Venezuelan lifeline and without electricity for all but a few hours a day, the economic hardships and human suffering in Cuba have dramatically worsened in recent months. Ousting Cuba’s government is a personal priority of Marco Rubio, Trump’s secretary of state and national security adviser, and would align with the “Donroe Doctrine,” an assertion of U.S. primacy in the Western Hemisphere.

For Trump, an attack on Cuba would be attractive because it would not require too much in the way of military assets and, at least in his mind, would solve a problem that has bedeviled U.S. presidents stretching back to the end of the Eisenhower administration. Trump might have already moved on Cuba had he not gotten bogged down in Iran. Of course, as the political scientist Alexander Downes has shown, using force to achieve regime change rarely works and often leads to unpredictable consequences—in the case of Cuba, it might mean a new influx of refugees to the United States.

But a lame-duck Trump could see it as a gamble worth taking on the off chance that he can be the president to finally unseat the communist regime. When it comes to foreign policy, Trump loves nothing more than attempting to hack open a Gordian knot. He might also view regime change in Cuba as a way to distract from his failure in Iran.

Another possibility is that Trump will focus his policy efforts abroad on enriching his family. He has already created ample opportunities for foreign entities to seemingly buy influence in his administration. Trump and his family members have accepted a jetliner from the emir of Qatar, a $2 billion investment from a fund led by the Saudi crown prince, and profits from a number of cryptocurrency enterprises, much of which came from foreign buyers. According to The New Yorker, Trump’s family has reaped $4 billion through such profiteering. Trump, secure in the knowledge that the Senate will not have the 67 votes it needs to remove him from office, might figure that he has nothing to lose in pursuing more moneymaking schemes.

The most surprising foreign policy option Trump could take during his lame-duck period, however, is doing nothing at all. The president has busied himself with remaking Washington, D.C., in his own image by redecorating the Oval Office, designing a White House ballroom, and attempting to remodel the Kennedy Center. There is a chance that he could spend his last two years in office issuing pardons, trying to prosecute political enemies on the side, and erecting monuments to himself. Given the alternatives, inaction on foreign policy may well be the best-case scenario for Trump’s lame-duck years.
